Unlike existing blockchain-based EHR access control systems that expose user identities on-chain or lack cryptographic privacy guarantees, ZK-EHR decouples authorization from identity disclosure by integrating zk-SNARK-based proofs with blockchain smart contracts to verify policy compliance without revealing user roles, affiliations, or credentials. The framework employs three differentiated actor roles\u2014Patient (Data Owner), Doctor (Care Provider), and Researcher (Authorized Analyst)\u2014with distinct policy-driven access workflows, a custom Groth16 zero-knowledge circuit for role-based constraint enforcement, and a modular architecture combining on-chain verification with off-chain encrypted storage via IPFS. Concrete design proposals for access revocation and replay attack prevention are introduced to address operational security requirements. The system was evaluated under multiple operational and adversarial scenarios. Experimental results indicate consistent on-chain verification latency (approximately 390 ms), reliable rejection of tampered submissions, and per-verification gas consumption of 216,631 gas. A comparative analysis against representative baseline systems demonstrates that ZK-EHR uniquely combines identity anonymity, on-chain cryptographic policy enforcement, and auditable encrypted record retrieval. These findings establish the feasibility of zk-SNARK-based access control for decentralized, verifiable, and privacy-aware EHR management.\nmeta-dc.format: application/pdf\nmeta-dc.identifier: 10.3390/systems14040385\nmeta-dc.language: en\nmeta-dc.publisher: Multidisciplinary Digital Publishing Institute\nmeta-dc.rights: http://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/3.0/\nmeta-dc.source: Systems 2026, Vol. 14, Page 385\nmeta-dc.subject: verifiable computing\nmeta-dc.title: Zero-Knowledge-Based Policy Enforcement for Privacy-Preserving Cross-Institutional Health Data Sharing on Blockchain\nmeta-dc.type: Article\nmeta-description: This study presents ZK-EHR, a decentralized access control framework designed to enable secure and privacy-preserving sharing of encrypted electronic health records across institutional boundaries. Unlike existing blockchain-based EHR access control systems that expose user identities on-chain or lack cryptographic privacy guarantees, ZK-EHR decouples authorization from identity disclosure by integrating zk-SNARK-based proofs with blockchain smart contracts to verify policy compliance without revealing user roles, affiliations, or credentials. The framework employs three differentiated actor roles\u2014Patient (Data Owner), Doctor (Care Provider), and Researcher (Authorized Analyst)\u2014with distinct policy-driven access workflows, a custom Groth16 zero-knowledge circuit for role-based constraint enforcement, and a modular architecture combining on-chain verification with off-chain encrypted storage via IPFS. Concrete design proposals for access revocation and replay attack prevention are introduced to address operational security requirements. The system was evaluated under multiple operational and adversarial scenarios. Experimental results indicate consistent on-chain verification latency (approximately 390 ms), reliable rejection of tampered submissions, and per-verification gas consumption of 216,631 gas. A comparative analysis against representative baseline systems demonstrates that ZK-EHR uniquely combines identity anonymity, on-chain cryptographic policy enforcement, and auditable encrypted record retrieval. Unlike existing blockchain-based EHR access control systems that expose user identities on-chain or lack cryptographic privacy guarantees, ZK-EHR decouples authorization from identity disclosure by integrating zk-SNARK-based proofs with blockchain smart contracts to verify policy compliance without revealing user roles, affiliations, or credentials. The framework employs three differentiated actor roles\u2014Patient (Data Owner), Doctor (Care Provider), and Researcher (Authorized Analyst)\u2014with distinct policy-driven access workflows, a custom Groth16 zero-knowledge circuit for role-based constraint enforcement, and a modular architecture combining on-chain verification with off-chain encrypted storage via IPFS.         "text": "The objective of this workshop was to discuss elements of Secure Processing Environments (SPEs). It brought together experts from academia, industry, and policy makers to exchange on the basic technical and organisational requirements, best practices, and their relevance for the realisation of the European Health Data Space (EHDS) and in connection with recent developments within the European Open Science Cloud (EOSC). Following presentations on OpenVRE and SecureVRE (Spain), Sensitive Cloud (Czech Republic), TSD (Norway), Molgenis (The Netherlands), Health Data Hub (France), de.NBI cloud (Germany), THL and Findata (both Finland), speakers agreed that many elements are indeed in place, according to these best practices. Kobling av helsedatakilder er s\u00e6rlig verdifullt i kunnskapsutviklingen. Tilgang til data og skreddersydd \u00b7 statistikk i sanntid er s\u00e6rlig viktig i beredskapssammenheng og ved innf\u00f8ring av nye legemidler. \u00c5 f\u00e5 tilgang til helsedata kan i dag ta lang tid. Tilgangen til helsedata m\u00e5 bli mer effektiv. Innhold og kanal for tilgjengeliggj\u00f8ring m\u00e5 tilpasses behov og bruksomr\u00e5de. Vi m\u00e5 utnytte mulighetene \u00b7 som ligger i \u00e5 tilgjengeliggj\u00f8re anonym statistikk og tilgjengeliggj\u00f8re data via trygge analyserom. ... Omfanget av helsetjenester \u00f8ker. Kompleksiteten og kostnader \u00f8ker ogs\u00e5, s\u00e6rlig i kommunal helse- og omsorgstjeneste. \n\nTilgangen p\u00e5 data om de \u00b7 kommunale helse- og omsorgstjenestene reflekterer ikke utviklingen. Vi ser \u00f8kende mangel p\u00e5 helsepersonell. 58 % av \u00e5rsverkene i helsetjenesten har \u00b7\n\nInnsamling av helsedata m\u00e5 bli mer effektiv. Rapportering tar tid fra helsepersonell som trengs, og burde brukes til pasientbehandling. Utvalget av \u00b7 data som samles inn m\u00e5 v\u00e6re relevant. Hvilke data som samles inn og i hvilket format, b\u00f8r revideres regelmessig og kritisk opp mot helsetjenestenes \u00b7 utvikling og samfunnets kunnskapsbehov. Der det er mulig og kostnadssvarende b\u00f8r data h\u00f8stes direkte og automatisert fra strukturerte \u00b7 elektroniske pasientjournaler og andre fagsystemer. Helsepersonell kan spares for arbeid og samfunnet for lagrings- og milj\u00f8kostnader ved at \u00b7 lovbestemte helseregistre fungerer som n\u00f8kkelregistre, slik at vi kan dele data mellom datasamlingene (gjenbruk av data) fremfor parallell ... Rugjer Boshkovikj 16, Skopje, North Macedonia\n\n2Sorsix, Skopje, North Macedonia\n\n3Zan Mitrev Clinic, Skopje, North Macedonia\n\n4Adagon, Skopje, North Macedonia\n\n5Ministry of Health, Skopje, North Macedonia\n\n6Jo\u017eef Stefan Institute, Ljubljana, Slovenia\n\n### Corresponding Author:\n\nMonika Simjanoska Misheva, PhD, Prof Dr\n\n### Abstract\n\nThe integration of artificial intelligence (AI) into clinical workflows is advancing even before full compliance with the European Union Cross-Border eHealth Network (MyHealth@EU) framework is achieved. While AI-based clinical decision support systems are automatically classified as high risk under the European Union\u2019s AI Act, cross-border health data exchange must also satisfy MyHealth@EU interoperability requirements. This creates a dual-compliance challenge: vertical safety and ethics controls mandated by the AI Act and horizontal semantic transport requirements enforced through Open National Contact Point (OpenNCP) gateways, many of which are still maturing toward production readiness. This paper provides a practical, phase-oriented tutorial that enables developers and providers to embed AI Act safeguards before approaching MyHealth@EU interoperability tests. The goal is to show how AI-specific metadata can be included in the Health Level Seven International Clinical Document Architecture and Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources messages without disrupting standard structures, ensuring both compliance and trustworthiness in AI-assisted clinical decisions. We systematically analyzed Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 (AI Act) and the OpenNCP technical specifications, extracting a harmonized set of overlapping obligations. The AI Act provisions on transparency, provenance, and robustness are mapped directly onto MyHealth@EU workflows, identifying the points where outgoing messages must record AI involvement, log provenance, and trigger validation. To operationalize this mapping, we propose a minimal extension set, covering AI contribution status, rationale, risk classification, and Annex IV documentation links, together with a phase-based compliance checklist that aligns AI Act controls with MyHealth@EU conformance steps. A simulated International Patient Summary transmission demonstrates how Clinical Document Architecture/Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources extensions can annotate AI involvement, how OpenNCP processes such enriched payloads, and how clinicians in another member state view the result with backward compatibility preserved. We expand on security considerations (eg, Open Worldwide Application Security Project generative AI risks such as prompt injection and adversarial inputs), continuous postmarket risk assessment, monitoring, and alignment with MyHealth@EU\u2019s incident aggregation system. Limitations reflect the immaturity of current infrastructures and regulations, with real-world validation pending the rollout of key dependencies. AI-enabled clinical software succeeds only when AI Act safeguards and MyHealth@EU interoperability rules are engineered together from *day 0*. This tutorial provides developers with a forward-looking blueprint that reduces duplication of effort, streamlines conformance testing, and embeds compliance early. While the concept is still in its early phases of practice, it represents a necessary and worthwhile direction for ensuring that future AI-enabled clinical systems can meet both European Union regulatory requirements from day 1.\n\nrisks such as prompt injection and adversarial inputs), continuous postmarket risk assessment, monitoring, and alignment with MyHealth@EU\u2019s incident aggregation system. Limitations reflect the immaturity of current infrastructures and regulations, with real-world validation pending the rollout of key dependencies.\n\n**J Med Internet Res 2025;27:e81184**  \n  \n[doi:10.2196/81184](https://doi.org/10.2196/81184)  \n  \n\n### Keywords\n\n[AI Act](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=AI%20Act&precise=true);\u00a0[MyHealth@EU](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=MyHealth@EU&precise=true);\u00a0[OpenNCP](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=OpenNCP&precise=true);\u00a0[HL7 CDA](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=HL7%20CDA&precise=true);\u00a0[HL7 FHIR](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=HL7%20FHIR&precise=true);\u00a0[Fast Healthcare Interoperability Resources](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=Fast%20Healthcare%20Interoperability%20Resources&precise=true);\u00a0[generative AI](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=generative%20AI&precise=true);\u00a0[interoperability](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=interoperability&precise=true);\u00a0[trustworthiness](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=trustworthiness&precise=true);\u00a0[reliability](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=reliability&precise=true);\u00a0[compliance](https://www.jmir.org/search?type=keyword&term=compliance&precise=true)\n\n  \n\n### Introduction\n\nWith increasing artificial intelligence (AI), and particularly generative artificial intelligence (GenAI) usage, medical software systems are rapidly evolving toward integrating AI directly into clinical workflows. This shift means many such systems will be categorized as high risk under the European Union\u2019s (EU) AI Act, requiring strict controls over multiple dimensions, including risk management, explainability, and postmarket monitoring [[1](#ref1)]. At the same time, the EU has built a horizontal compliance and interoperability infrastructure through the European Union Cross-Border eHealth Network (MyHealth@EU), which mandates how structured health data flow between member states, passing through the Open National Contact Point (OpenNCP) software\u2019s security gateways, and preserving semantic integrity [[2](#ref2)]. The result is a dual-compliance framework: the AI Act regulates the functionality, that is, what ",

It covers\n   106\tcontent inventory, text extraction vs. page rasterization, embedded\n   107\tcontent extraction, and document-type-aware reading strategies.\n   108\t\n   109\tFor PDF form filling, creation, merging, splitting, or watermarking,\n   110\tgo read `/mnt/skills/public/pdf/SKILL.md`.\n   111\t\n   112\t---\n   113\t\n   114\t## DOCX / DOC\n   115\t\n   116\tThe `docx` skill covers editing, creating, tracked changes, images.\n   117\tRead it if you need any of those. For a quick look:\n   118\t\n   119\t```bash\n   120\textract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/memo.docx | head -200\n   121\t```\n   122\t\n   123\tLegacy `.doc` (not `.docx`) must be converted first \u2014 see the `docx`\n   124\tskill.\n   125\t\n   126\t---\n   127\t\n   128\t## XLSX / XLS / spreadsheets\n   129\t\n   130\tThe `xlsx` skill covers formulas, formatting, charts, creating. Read\n   131\tit if you need any of those. For a quick look at an `.xlsx`:\n   132\t\n   133\t```bash\n   134\textract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.xlsx | head -100\n   135\t```\n   136\t\n   137\tFor `.xlsm`, add `--format xlsx` (same zip structure; only the\n   138\textension differs). When you need a structured preview in Python:\n   139\t\n   140\t```python\n   141\tfrom openpyxl import load_workbook\n   142\twb = load_workbook(\"/mnt/user-data/uploads/data.xlsx\", read_only=True)\n   143\tprint(\"Sheets:\", wb.sheetnames)\n   144\tws = wb.active\n   145\tfor row in ws.iter_rows(max_row=5, values_only=True):\n   146\t    print(row)\n   147\t```\n   148\t\n   149\t`read_only=True` matters \u2014 without it, openpyxl loads the entire\n   150\tworkbook into memory, which breaks on large files. Do not trust\n   151\t`ws.max_row` in read-only mode: many non-Excel writers omit the\n   152\tdimension record, so it comes back `None` or wrong. If you need a row\n   153\tcount, iterate or use pandas.\n   154\t\n   155\t**Legacy `.xls`** \u2014 openpyxl raises `InvalidFileException`. Use:\n   156\t\n   157\t```python\n   158\timport pandas as pd\n   159\tdf = pd.read_excel(\"/mnt/user-data/uploads/old.xls\", engine=\"xlrd\", nrows=5)\n   160\t```\n   161\t\n   162\t**`.ods` (OpenDocument)** \u2014 openpyxl also rejects this. Use:\n   163\t\n   164\t```python\n   165\timport pandas as pd\n   166\tdf = pd.read_excel(\"/mnt/user-data/uploads/data.ods\", engine=\"odf\", nrows=5)\n   167\t```\n   168\t\n   169\t---\n   170\t\n   171\t## PPTX\n   172\t\n   173\t```bash\n   174\textract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/deck.pptx | head -200\n   175\t```\n   176\t\n   177\t**Legacy `.ppt`** \u2014 convert to `.pptx` first via LibreOffice; see\n   178\t`/mnt/skills/public/pptx/SKILL.md` for the sandbox-safe\n   179\t`scripts/office/soffice.py` wrapper (bare `soffice` hangs here because\n   180\tthe seccomp filter blocks the `AF_UNIX` sockets LibreOffice uses for\n   181\tinstance management).\n   182\t\n   183\tFor anything beyond reading, go to `/mnt/skills/public/pptx/SKILL.md`.\n   184\t\n   185\t---\n   186\t\n   187\t## CSV / TSV\n   188\t\n   189\t**Do not** `cat` or `head` these blindly. A CSV with a 50KB quoted cell\n   190\tin row 1 will wreck your `head -5`. Use pandas with `nrows`:\n   191\t\n   192\t```python\n   193\timport pandas as pd\n   194\tdf = pd.read_csv(\"/mnt/user-data/uploads/data.csv\", nrows=5)\n   195\tprint(df)\n   196\tprint()\n   197\tprint(df.dtypes)\n   198\t```\n   199\t\n   200\tApproximate row count without loading (over-counts if the file has\n   201\tRFC-4180 quoted newlines \u2014 the same quoted-cell case this section\n   202\twarned about above):\n   203\t\n   204\t```bash\n   205\twc -l /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.csv\n   206\t```\n   207\t\n   208\tFull analysis only after you know the shape:\n   209\t\n   210\t```python\n   211\tdf = pd.read_csv(\"/mnt/user-data/uploads/data.csv\")\n   212\tprint(df.describe())\n   213\t```\n   214\t\n   215\tTSV: same, with `sep=\"\\t\"`.\n   216\t\n   217\t---\n   218\t\n   219\t## JSON / JSONL\n   220\t\n   221\tStructure first, content second:\n   222\t\n   223\t```bash\n   224\tjq 'type' /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.json\n   225\tjq 'if type == \"array\" then length elif type == \"object\" then keys else . end' /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.json\n   226\t```\n   227\t\n   228\t(`keys` errors on scalar JSON roots \u2014 a bare `\"hello\"` or `42` is valid\n   229\tJSON per RFC 7159 \u2014 so guard the branch.)\n   230\t\n   231\tThen drill into what the user actually asked about.\n   232\t\n   233\tJSONL (one object per line) \u2014 do **not** `jq` the whole file; work line\n   234\tby line:\n   235\t\n   236\t```bash\n   237\thead -3 /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.jsonl | jq .\n   238\twc -l /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.jsonl\n   239\t```\n   240\t\n   241\t---\n   242\t\n   243\t## Images (JPG / PNG / GIF / WEBP)\n   244\t\n   245\t**You can already see uploaded images.** They are injected into your\n   246\tcontext as vision inputs alongside the `<uploaded_files>` pointer. You\n   247\tdo not need to read them from disk to describe them.\n   248\t\n   249\tThe disk copy is only needed if you are going to **process** the image\n   250\tprogrammatically:\n   251\t\n   252\t```python\n   253\tfrom PIL import Image\n   254\timg = Image.open(\"/mnt/user-data/uploads/photo.jpg\")\n   255\tprint(img.size, img.mode, img.format)\n   256\t```\n   257\t\n   258\tFor OCR on an image (text extraction, not description):\n   259\t\n   260\t```python\n   261\timport pytesseract\n   262\tprint(pytesseract.image_to_string(img))\n   263\t```\n   264\t\n   265\tNote: the client resizes images larger than 2000\u00d72000 down to that\n   266\tbound and re-encodes as JPEG before upload, so the disk copy may not\n   267\tbe the user's original bytes. For most processing this doesn't matter;\n   268\tif the user is asking about original-resolution pixel data, flag it.\n   269\t\n   270\t---\n   271\t\n   272\t## Archives (ZIP / TAR / TAR.GZ)\n   273\t\n   274\t**List first. Extract never \u2014 unless the user explicitly asks.**\n   275\tArchives can be huge, contain path traversal, or nest forever.\n   276\t\n   277\t```bash\n   278\tunzip -l /mnt/user-data/uploads/bundle.zip\n   279\ttar -tf /mnt/user-data/uploads/bundle.tar\n   280\t```\n   281\t\n   282\tGNU tar auto-detects compression \u2014 `tar -tf` works on `.tar`,\n   283\t`.tar.gz`, `.tar.bz2`, `.tar.xz` alike. Don't hard-code `-z`.\n   284\t\n   285\tIf the user wants one file from inside, extract just that one:\n   286\t\n   287\t```bash\n   288\tunzip -p /mnt/user-data/uploads/bundle.zip path/inside/file.txt\n   289\t```\n   290\t\n   291\t**Standalone `.gz`** (not a tar) compresses a single file \u2014 there is\n   292\tno manifest to list. Just peek at the decompressed content:\n   293\t\n   294\t```bash\n   295\tzcat /mnt/user-data/uploads/data.json.gz | head -50\n   296\t```\n   297\t\n   298\t---\n   299\t\n   300\t## EPUB / ODT\n   301\t\n   302\t```bash\n   303\textract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/book.epub | head -200\n   304\t```\n   305\t\n   306\tFor long ebooks, pipe through `head` \u2014 you rarely need the whole thing\n   307\tto answer a question.\n   308\t\n   309\t---\n   310\t\n   311\t## RTF / IPYNB\n   312\t\n   313\t```bash\n   314\textract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/notes.rtf | head -200\n   315\textract-text /mnt/user-data/uploads/notebook.ipynb | head -200\n   316\t```\n   317\t\n   318\t---\n   319\t\n   320\t## Plain text / code / logs\n   321\t\n   322\tCheck the size first:\n   323\t\n   324\t```bash\n   325\twc -c /mnt/user-data/uploads/app.log\n   326\t```\n   327\t\n   328\t- **Under ~20KB**: `cat` is fine.\n   329\t- **Over ~20KB**: `head -100` and `tail -100` to orient. If the user\n   330\t  asked about something specific, `grep` for it. Load the whole thing\n   331\t  only if you genuinely need all of it.\n   332\t\n   333\tFor log files, the user almost always cares about the end:\n   334\t\n   335\t```bash\n   336\ttail -200 /mnt/user-data/uploads/app.log\n   337\t```\n   338\t\n   339\t---\n   340\t\n   341\t## Unknown extension\n   342\t\n   343\t```bash\n   344\tfile /mnt/user-data/uploads/mystery.bin\n   345\txxd /mnt/user-data/uploads/mystery.bin | head -5\n   346\t```\n   347\t\n   348\t`file` identifies most things. `xxd` head shows magic bytes. If `file`\n   349\tsays \"data\" and the hex doesn't match anything you recognize, ask the\n   350\tuser what it is instead of guessing.\n   351\t",

All sources of\\ninformation have been properly cited according to academic standards.\\n\\n### Statement of Original Contribution\\n\\nThis thesis presents independent and original research conducted as part\\nof the Master of Science in Sustainable Energy Logistics at Molde\\nUniversity College. It introduces VERA SNARK, a conceptual and\\narchitectural framework for verifiable and privacy preserving compliance\\nin health data exchange under the emerging European Health Data Space.\\n\\nThe originality of the work lies in translating the legal and ethical\\nprinciples of the General Data Protection Regulation into structured,\\nmachine verifiable cryptographic assertions. Rather than building a\\nproduction system or a new cryptographic primitive, the thesis defines\\nhow lawful processing, consent, purpose limitation, and the right to\\nerasure can be expressed as verifiable conditions within a zero\\nknowledge proof system. The research contributes the following\\ninnovations.\\n\\n**1. The Triple Assertion Circuit.**\\\\\\nA formalised model that evaluates authorization validity, active\\nconsent, and data minimisation as jointly verifiable predicates. This\\ndemonstrates how GDPR obligations can be represented as compliance\\nconditions suitable for succinct verification.\\n\\n**2. The Compliance Plane.**\\\\\\nA multi layer abstraction that links cryptographic verification with\\ninstitutional policy, cross border regulatory constraints, and\\ngovernance requirements. This provides a structured way to model how\\njurisdictional differences influence verifiable computation.\\n\\n**3. The Crypto Erasure by Invalidation mechanism.**\\\\\\nA conceptual method for representing the GDPR right to erasure by\\nmathematically invalidating proof references instead of deleting data.\\nThis offers a privacy preserving interpretation of revocation in\\nsettings where personal data are not directly processed.\\n\\n**4. The Green ZK Indicator.**\\\\\\nA preliminary sustainability metric for analysing energy use within\\nprivacy preserving proofs. It supports energy aware evaluation by\\nlinking cryptographic operations to approximate environmental cost.\\n\\n**5. The VERA Bench specification.**\\\\\\nA reproducible blueprint for evaluating proof latency, compliance\\nguarantees, and environmental behaviour once a complete implementation\\nis available. It includes synthetic FHIR datasets, scenario modelling,\\nand structured validation procedures.\\n\\n**6. The foundation of Zero Knowledge Governance Systems.**\\\\\\nA conceptual extension that demonstrates how lawful, ethical, and policy\\ndriven requirements can be expressed as verifiable governance artefacts,\\nenabling applications beyond healthcare such as finance, mobility,\\neducation, and energy.\\n\\nThese contributions form a verifiable specification rather than a\\ndeployed system. Validation is conducted through synthetic HL7 FHIR R5\\ndatasets, conformance fixtures, scenario based modelling, and\\nreproducible analysis scripts. This ensures transparency without\\nprocessing personal data. The work provides a multidisciplinary\\nsynthesis of cryptography, legal theory, governance design, and ethical\\nreasoning. It establishes a foundation for future applied research and\\nstandardisation efforts toward trustworthy digital health\\ninfrastructures.\\n\\nI affirm that this contribution is authentic, traceable, and developed\\nin accordance with responsible research principles.\\n\\n**Signed:** Arshad Akhtar Abbasia\\n\\n**Molde, Norway - 2025**\\n\\n## Acknowledgements & Ethical Declarations\\n\\nThis thesis began with the guidance of Professor Jo\\u00e3o Ferreira at Molde\\nUniversity College, whose openness and intellectual generosity shaped\\nthe direction and the spirit of this work. His early introduction to\\ndecentralised identifiers, verifiable credentials, multi-cloud\\narchitectures, and cross-border EHR research created the space in which\\nthe idea for VERA SNARK could emerge. For that spark and for his\\npatience, I am deeply grateful.\\n\\nI also thank the faculty and research mentors who encouraged depth,\\ndiscipline, and critical thinking during every step of this journey.\\nTheir insistence on methodological clarity and ethical responsibility\\nhelped transform uncertainty into structure and speculation into\\nverifiable reasoning.\\n\\nI extend sincere appreciation to colleagues and peers at Molde\\nUniversity College, as well as contributors and practitioners across the\\nEuropean privacy, blockchain, and digital governance communities. Their\\ninformal discussions, technical critique, and generous sharing of\\nknowledge offered both direction and confidence when the work moved into\\nunfamiliar territory.\\n\\nMy gratitude also goes to the open-source communities whose tools and\\nstandards provided the foundation for this research. The Circom and\\nHalo2 contributors, the broader zkSNARK ecosystem, and the HL7 FHIR\\ncommunity made it possible to study compliance and privacy without ever\\ninteracting with real personal data. Their commitment to openness\\nenabled this thesis to stand on transparent and verifiable ground.\\n\\nI am grateful to my family and close friends for their patience and\\nencouragement throughout long nights of reading, modelling, and\\nrewriting. Their support provided the stability that research quietly\\ndepends on.\\n\\nFinally, I acknowledge all unnamed voices who influenced this work\\nthrough conversation, critique, or curiosity. Each played a small but\\nmeaningful role in shaping the lens through which this research was\\ncarried out.\\n\\n# **Ethical Declarations**\\n\\n**Ethical Compliance**\\\\\\nThis research uses only synthetic, non identifiable datasets generated\\naccording to HL7 FHIR R5 schemas. No real patient data were accessed or\\nprocessed. The study follows the guidance of the Norwegian Centre for\\nResearch Data and the principles of transparency, fairness, and respect\\nfor data subjects' rights described in GDPR Recital 39.\\n\\n**Data Statement**\\\\\\nAll datasets are synthetic, publicly reproducible, and generated solely\\nfor methodological testing. No personal data were stored, transmitted,\\nor shared.\\n\\n**Conflict of Interest**\\\\\\nThe author declares no financial or personal conflict of interest that\\ncould influence the design, conduct, or interpretation of this work.\\n\\n**Funding Acknowledgement**\\\\\\nThis thesis was self initiated and received no external funding beyond\\ninstitutional academic support.\\n\\n**Open-Source and FAIR Commitment**\\\\\\nAll conceptual circuits, validation templates, and analysis scripts\\nfollow FAIR principles: findable, accessible, interoperable, and\\nreusable. Replication materials will be shared for academic\\nverification.\\n\\n**Sustainability Statement**\\\\\\nThe research follows energy-aware practices and contributes to\\ndiscussions on responsible computation within the broader perspective of\\nGreen ICT initiatives.\\n\\n**Integrity Statement**\\\\\\nAll claims, figures, and arguments in this thesis are grounded in\\nverifiable and traceable sources. No data, citation, or interpretation\\nhas been fabricated, suppressed, or manipulated. The work is guided by\\nthe principle that research must be accountable, reproducible, and\\nhumane.\\n\\n# **Closing Note**\\n\\nThis thesis is dedicated to the pursuit of verifiable truth and to the\\nbelief that digital systems must serve both accuracy and conscience. May\\nevery future implementation of this work continue to honour the same\\ncommitment to clarity, integrity, and respect.\\n\\n## Preface\\n\\nThis thesis grew out of an academic and personal journey that began long\\nbefore the research formally started. These systems demonstrated creativity\\nand technical ambition, yet they also revealed consistent gaps. The contribution integrates\n   263\tthree complementary components: the **Triple-Assertion Circuit** for\n   264\tevaluating compliance predicates, the **Compliance Plane** for modelling\n   265\tcross-border policy constraints, and the **Crypto-Erasure by\n   266\tInvalidation** mechanism for representing revocation as mathematical\n   267\tproof invalidation. Together, they outline a structured approach for\n   268\texpressing **regulatory obligations** as **succinct zero-knowledge\n   269\tproofs** without revealing personal data.\n   270\t\n   271\tThe work will follows a **design-science methodology** and will use\n   272\t**synthetic FHIR-compliant datasets**, formal scenario modelling, and\n   273\tstructured consistency checks to validate the **internal correctness**\n   274\tand **regulatory completeness** of the architecture. These simulations\n   275\tdo not constitute a cryptographic implementation. Instead, they\n   276\testablish the **behavioural expectations**, **compliance guarantees**,\n   277\tand **design boundaries** of a future practical system. The thesis also\n   278\tdevelops a **reproducible evaluation blueprint** that defines how\n   279\tlatency, compliance guarantees, and environmental considerations could\n   280\tbe assessed once a full implementation is available.\n   281\t\n   282\t**VERA-SNARK** provides a verifiable specification and governance\n   283\tarchitecture that demonstrates how lawful data processing can be\n   284\ttransformed into **privacy-preserving**, **machine-checkable evidence**.\n   285\tIt offers a forward-looking foundation for building **trustable digital\n   286\thealth infrastructures** under the European Health Data Space and\n   287\tcontributes to the emerging class of **zero-knowledge governance\n   288\tsystems**.\n   289\t\n   290\t**Keywords:** Zero-knowledge proofs; GDPR The idea of\\ndeveloping a framework that could express lawful processing as evidence\\nemerged naturally from examining where previous work struggled. The\\nconcept of VERA-SNARK did not appear suddenly. It grew from the\\ninteraction between those early architectural lessons and an older,\\nunfinished challenge I had once faced while working at Moonfit in 2022.\\nAt that time I explored how sensitive movement and health information\\ncould be handled privately in a move to earn system, but the available\\nprivacy technologies were not sufficient. The problem stayed unresolved,\\nand it resurfaced when I encountered Professor Jo\\u00e3o's guidance on\\ndecentralised architectures for cross-border health data.\\n\\nThis background created a clear purpose. If privacy in health data\\nsystems is to be meaningful, then consent, purpose limitation, and\\nerasure cannot remain policy statements alone. They must become\\nverifiable operations. This insight guided the development of VERA-SNARK\\nas a conceptual framework that translates these legal principles into\\ncryptographic assertions without requiring disclosure of personal data.\\nThe thesis follows a design-science methodology, uses synthetic FHIR\\ncompliant datasets, and relies on scenario modelling and structured\\nconsistency checks. The work does not present a cryptographic\\nimplementation. Instead, it explores architectural feasibility,\\nregulatory alignment, and the internal correctness of a system that\\ncould one day support verifiable health data governance across Europe.\\n\\nI am grateful to my supervisors and the faculty at Molde University\\nCollege for their guidance and encouragement. Their openness to\\ninterdisciplinary inquiry made it possible to explore a problem at the\\nintersection of cryptography, digital governance, and healthcare. I also\\nthank the colleagues and researchers whose discussions helped refine the\\nconceptual boundaries of this work. Any remaining limitations are my\\nown. I hope the ideas presented here contribute meaningfully to the\\nbroader effort of building trustworthy and privacy-respecting digital\\nhealth infrastructures under the European Health Data Space.\\n\\n**Arshad Akhtar Abbasia**\\\\\\n**Molde University College, Norway**\\\\\\n**Spring 2025**\\n\\n## Abstract\\n\\n**Title:** VERA-SNARK: Succinct Triple-Assertion Zero-Knowledge Proofs\\nfor FHIR-Integrated Health Data Exchange under the European Health Data\\nSpace (EHDS) with crypto erasure.\\n\\n**Abstract:**\\\\\\nThe secure and lawful exchange of **electronic health records** remains\\none of the most persistent challenges in European digital governance.\\nThe **European Health Data Space** provides a foundation for\\ncross-border health-data mobility, yet it does not offer technical\\nmechanisms to prove that data are accessed with **valid consent**,\\n**lawful purpose**, and **verifiable erasure** at the exact moment of\\nuse. Current **privacy-enhancing technologies** improve confidentiality\\nbut cannot express **GDPR obligations as machine-checkable evidence**.\\n\\nThis thesis introduces **VERA-SNARK**, a conceptual framework that\\ntransforms the core principles of **lawful processing**, **active\\nconsent**, **purpose limitation**, and the **right to erasure** into\\n**verifiable cryptographic assertions**. The contribution integrates\\nthree complementary components: the **Triple-Assertion Circuit** for\\nevaluating compliance predicates, the **Compliance Plane** for modelling\\ncross-border policy constraints, and the **Crypto-Erasure by\\nInvalidation** mechanism for representing revocation as mathematical\\nproof invalidation. Together, they outline a structured approach for\\nexpressing **regulatory obligations** as **succinct zero-knowledge\\nproofs** without revealing personal data.\\n\\nThe work will follows a **design-science methodology** and will use\\n**synthetic FHIR-compliant datasets**, formal scenario modelling, and\\nstructured consistency checks to validate the **internal correctness**\\nand **regulatory completeness** of the architecture. These simulations\\ndo not constitute a cryptographic implementation. Instead, they\\nestablish the **behavioural expectations**, **compliance guarantees**,\\nand **design boundaries** of a future practical system. The thesis also\\ndevelops a **reproducible evaluation blueprint** that defines how\\nlatency, compliance guarantees, and environmental considerations could\\nbe assessed once a full implementation is available.\\n\\n**VERA-SNARK** provides a verifiable specification and governance\\narchitecture that demonstrates how lawful data processing can be\\ntransformed into **privacy-preserving**, **machine-checkable evidence**.\\nIt offers a forward-looking foundation for building **trustable digital\\nhealth infrastructures** under the European Health Data Space and\\ncontributes to the emerging class of **zero-knowledge governance\\nsystems**.\\n\\n**Keywords:** Zero-knowledge proofs; GDPR compliance; European Health\\nData Space; verifiable consent; crypto-erasure; FHIR; zkSNARK; compliant\\ncomputation; privacy-preserving auditability; digital governance.\\n\\n## Table of Contents\\n\\n### Front Matter\\n\\ni\\u2003\\u2003Title Page\\\\\\nii\\u2003\\u2003Statement of Original Audits typically rely on\n   552\tdatabase logs and institutional trust, which are difficult to reconcile\n   553\tacross borders or between cloud providers (Bunn & Graham, 2023).\n   554\t\n   555\tParallel progress in **privacy-enhancing technologies (PETs)**,\n   556\tparticularly **zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs)**, has introduced the\n   557\tpossibility of verifying statements about data without revealing the\n   558\tdata themselves (Heiss et al., 2024). Recent work demonstrates that\n   559\t**succinct non-interactive arguments of knowledge (zkSNARKs)** can\n   560\tefficiently prove correctness of computation while maintaining\n   561\tconfidentiality (B\u00fcnz et al., 2024). However, most ZKP deployments focus\n   562\ton blockchain scalability or digital identity, not on codifying legal\n   563\tpredicates such as consent validity or data-minimisation obligations\n   564\t(Kohlweiss & Parno, 2025).\n   565\t\n   566\tThe ethical motivation for this study arises from the same dilemma\n   567\tconfronting regulators: how to balance transparency with privacy in a\n   568\tdata-driven society. Verification should not entail exposure, and\n   569\tprivacy should not exclude accountability. This thesis introduces\n   570\t**VERA-SNARK**, a framework that transforms compliance from a\n   571\tdeclarative process into a verifiable act, embedding legal and ethical\n   572\treasoning into cryptographic logic.\n   573\t\n   574\tThe underlying belief is that trustworthy digital governance must be\n   575\tmeasurable. The ability to verify without intrusion represents not only\n   576\ta technical advancement but also a moral necessity for sustaining public\n   577\ttrust in the European data economy.\n   578\t\n   579\t### **References**\n   580\t\n   581\t-   B\u00fcnz, B., Lyubashevsky, V., & Zhou, R. (2023). *The GDPR -- A\n   599\t    Commentary (2nd ed.).* Oxford University Press.\n   600\t\n   601\t## **1.2 Problem Statement**\n   602\t\n   603\tThe transformation of healthcare data governance within the European\n   604\tUnion remains incomplete. The **European Health Data Space (EHDS)**\n   605\tRegulation, adopted in 2025, establishes the first legal framework for\n   606\tcross-border health data use, yet it does not prescribe how compliance\n   607\tshould be technically verified in real time (European Commission, 2025).\n   608\tMember states, hospitals, and research institutions continue to rely on\n   609\tfragmented infrastructures and heterogeneous consent models (Costa et\n   610\tal., 2024). The result is a compliance environment that depends on trust\n   611\tand documentation rather than on verifiable mathematical assurance\n   612\t(Kuner et al., 2023).\n   613\t\n   614\tAt present, there is no technical standard capable of proving that an\n   615\tindividual clinician's data access satisfies all three core principles\n   616\tof lawful processing under the GDPR: authorization, active consent, and\n   617\tdata minimization. Once information leaves its source repository, there\n   618\tis no cryptographic mechanism to demonstrate that only the minimal\n   619\trequired data were used or that access remains within the limits of the\n   620\tpatient's consent (Bunn & Graham, 2023). Revocation of consent---central\n   621\tto the \"right to be forgotten\" under Article 17 of the GDPR---relies on\n   622\tmanual deletion and administrative enforcement rather than automatic\n   623\tinvalidation at the proof level (Kuner et al., 2023).\n   624\t\n   625\tAlthough **zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs)** and related privacy-enhancing\n   626\ttechnologies have matured rapidly, most implementations are designed for\n   627\tblockchain scalability or digital identity verification (Heiss et al.,\n   628\t2024; B\u00fcnz et al., 2024). None address the legal semantics of consent or\n   629\tthe requirement for simultaneous verification of authorization and\n   630\tminimization (Kohlweiss & Parno, 2025). No integrated framework\n   631\tcurrently connects the mathematical logic of ZKPs with the institutional\n   632\tlogic of governance and regulatory oversight.\n   633\t\n   634\tThis gap forms the central research problem of the thesis. Healthcare\n   635\tsystems lack a verifiable architecture that can provide continuous,\n   636\tmachine-readable proof that each data transaction complies with GDPR and\n   637\tEHDS obligations. The absence of such a mechanism limits ethical\n   638\tinteroperability, erodes patient trust, and exposes institutions to\n   639\tlegal uncertainty.\n   640\t\n   641\tThe **VERA-SNARK** framework is conceived to address this deficiency. It\n   642\tproposes a **Triple-Assertion Circuit** that verifies authorization,\n   643\tconsent, and minimization within a unified zkSNARK structure, together\n   644\twith a mechanism for **crypto-erasure by invalidation** that\n   645\tcryptographically nullifies proofs upon consent withdrawal. By\n   646\ttransforming compliance obligations into verifiable computations, the\n   647\tframework seeks to close the long-standing gap between legal intent and\n   648\ttechnical enforcement, advancing a model where privacy, accountability,\n   649\tand efficiency coexist.\n   650\t\n   651\t### **References**\n   652\t\n   653\t-   B\u00fcnz, B., Lyubashevsky, V., & Zhou, R. The work seeks to translate the legal\n   678\trequirements of the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) into a\n   679\tcryptographically verifiable system capable of proving compliance rather\n   680\tthan merely asserting it (European Commission, 2025; Kuner et al.,\n   681\t2023). In doing so, the study introduces **VERA-SNARK**---a\n   682\tdesign-science framework demonstrating that accountability, privacy, and\n   683\tefficiency can coexist through mathematical proof.\n   684\t\n   685\tThe research adopts the design-science paradigm, where artefacts are\n   686\tdeveloped to test and refine theory (Hevner & Gregor, 2023). Each\n   687\tobjective therefore links a technical outcome to a normative principle\n   688\tof ethics, transparency, and sustainability.\n   689\t\n   690\t### **Specific Objectives**\n   691\t\n   692\t1.  **To formalize the Triple-Assertion Circuit.** Define a\n   693\t    zkSNARK-based logic verifying three concurrent conditions of lawful\n\t< truncated lines 694-757 >\n   758\t    Research for Societal Impact.* *Information Systems Journal,* 33(6),\n   759\t    1279--1302.\n   760\t-   Kohlweiss, A., & Parno, B. (2025). *Succinct Compliance Proofs and\n   761\t    Privacy Audits.* *IEEE Security & Privacy,* 23(1), 33--42.\n   762\t-   Kuner, C., Bygrave, L. A., & Docksey, C. (2023). *The GDPR -- A\n   763\t    Commentary (2nd ed.).* Oxford University Press.\n   764\t\n   765\t## **1.4 Significance of the Study**\n   766\t\n   767\tThe significance of this study lies in its effort to convert European\n   768\tprivacy and governance principles into verifiable computation for real\n   769\thealth-data exchange. The **European Health Data Space (EHDS)**\n   770\tRegulation, adopted in 2025, defines governance, access bodies, and\n   771\treuse frameworks but does not prescribe any mechanism for real-time\n   772\tverification of lawful processing (European Commission, 2025). This\n   773\tleaves a persistent gap between legal intention and machine-verifiable\n   774\tenforcement. **VERA-SNARK** addresses this gap by introducing a proof\n   775\tlayer that binds authorization, active consent, and data minimisation to\n   776\tevery transaction, producing evidence that can be independently verified\n   777\twithout revealing personal information.\n   778\t\n   779\tFrom an **academic perspective**, this research extends\n   780\t**privacy-enhancing technologies (PETs)** into the domain of normative\n   781\tgovernance. Contemporary surveys describe homomorphic encryption, secure\n   782\tmultiparty computation, trusted execution environments, differential\n   783\tprivacy, federated learning, and synthetic data as the primary tools for\n   784\tprotecting information within European data spaces (Heiss et al., 2024;\n   785\tvan den Hoven et al., 2024). These approaches ensure confidentiality but\n   786\tdo not encode **legal predicates** as cryptographic statements that\n   787\tauditors can verify without access to raw data. *VERA-SNARK* contributes\n   788\ta law-aware proof object that allows such verification ex post, creating\n   789\ta new bridge between computational privacy and institutional\n   790\taccountability.\n   791\t\n   792\tFrom a **technological standpoint**, the study complements rather than\n   793\treplaces existing **interoperability standards**. The HL7 FHIR R5\n   794\tframework, released in 2023, standardises healthcare data models and\n   795\tAPIs, while **MyHealth@EU** enables cross-border services for patient\n   796\tsummaries and e-prescriptions (Costa et al., 2024). Neither provides a\n   797\tmethod to prove that access events fulfil GDPR's conditions for lawful\n   798\tprocessing. *VERA-SNARK* defines that missing profile by attaching\n   799\tzero-knowledge proof semantics to FHIR artefacts, allowing compliance to\n   800\ttravel with the data itself for verification and audit.\n   801\t\n   802\tAt the ecosystem level, this work aligns with **self-sovereign identity\n   803\t(SSI)** and **verifiable credentials (VCs)** developed under the\n   804\tEuropean Blockchain Services Infrastructure (EBSI).       "json_block": "{\"language\": \"plaintext\", \"code\": \"every data transaction satisfies Articles 5, 6, 7, and 17 concerning\\nfairness, consent, minimisation, and erasure. Audits typically rely on\\ndatabase logs and institutional trust, which are difficult to reconcile\\nacross borders or between cloud providers (Bunn & Graham, 2023).\\n\\nParallel progress in **privacy-enhancing technologies (PETs)**,\\nparticularly **zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs)**, has introduced the\\npossibility of verifying statements about data without revealing the\\ndata themselves (Heiss et al., 2024). Recent work demonstrates that\\n**succinct non-interactive arguments of knowledge (zkSNARKs)** can\\nefficiently prove correctness of computation while maintaining\\nconfidentiality (B\\u00fcnz et al., 2024). However, most ZKP deployments focus\\non blockchain scalability or digital identity, not on codifying legal\\npredicates such as consent validity or data-minimisation obligations\\n(Kohlweiss & Parno, 2025).\\n\\nThe ethical motivation for this study arises from the same dilemma\\nconfronting regulators: how to balance transparency with privacy in a\\ndata-driven society. Verification should not entail exposure, and\\nprivacy should not exclude accountability. This thesis introduces\\n**VERA-SNARK**, a framework that transforms compliance from a\\ndeclarative process into a verifiable act, embedding legal and ethical\\nreasoning into cryptographic logic.\\n\\nThe underlying belief is that trustworthy digital governance must be\\nmeasurable. The ability to verify without intrusion represents not only\\na technical advancement but also a moral necessity for sustaining public\\ntrust in the European data economy.\\n\\n### **References**\\n\\n-   B\\u00fcnz, B., Lyubashevsky, V., & Zhou, R. (2024). *Aggregated Plonk:\\n    Succinct Zero-Knowledge Proofs for Complex Workflows.* Proceedings\\n    of the Privacy Enhancing Technologies Symposium (PETS).\\n-   Bunn, S., & Graham, M. (2023). *Cross-Border Data Compliance and\\n    Cloud Governance in the EU Health Sector.* Computer Law & Security\\n    Review, 52, 105756.\\n-   Costa, A., Fern\\u00e1ndez, S., & Weber, T. (2024). *Operational Readiness\\n    for the European Health Data Space: A Comparative Study of Member\\n    State Frameworks.* Health Policy and Technology, 13(2), 100768.\\n-   European Commission. (2024). *Zero-Knowledge Proofs for\\n    Responsible AI and Data Sharing.* Journal of Privacy and Digital\\n    Ethics, 6(1), 22-37.\\n-   Kohlweiss, A., & Parno, B. (2025). *Succinct Compliance Proofs and\\n    Privacy Audits.* IEEE Security & Privacy, 23(1), 33-42.\\n-   Kuner, C., Bygrave, L. A., & Docksey, C. (2023). *The GDPR -- A\\n    Commentary (2nd ed.).* Oxford University Press.\\n\\n## **1.2 Problem Statement**\\n\\nThe transformation of healthcare data governance within the European\\nUnion remains incomplete. The **European Health Data Space (EHDS)**\\nRegulation, adopted in 2025, establishes the first legal framework for\\ncross-border health data use, yet it does not prescribe how compliance\\nshould be technically verified in real time (European Commission, 2025).\\nMember states, hospitals, and research institutions continue to rely on\\nfragmented infrastructures and heterogeneous consent models (Costa et\\nal., 2024). The result is a compliance environment that depends on trust\\nand documentation rather than on verifiable mathematical assurance\\n(Kuner et al., 2023).\\n\\nAt present, there is no technical standard capable of proving that an\\nindividual clinician's data access satisfies all three core principles\\nof lawful processing under the GDPR: authorization, active consent, and\\ndata minimization. Once information leaves its source repository, there\\nis no cryptographic mechanism to demonstrate that only the minimal\\nrequired data were used or that access remains within the limits of the\\npatient's consent (Bunn & Graham, 2023). Revocation of consent---central\\nto the \\\"right to be forgotten\\\" under Article 17 of the GDPR---relies on\\nmanual deletion and administrative enforcement rather than automatic\\ninvalidation at the proof level (Kuner et al., 2023).\\n\\nAlthough **zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs)** and related privacy-enhancing\\ntechnologies have matured rapidly, most implementations are designed for\\nblockchain scalability or digital identity verification (Heiss et al.,\\n2024; B\\u00fcnz et al., 2024). None address the legal semantics of consent or\\nthe requirement for simultaneous verification of authorization and\\nminimization (Kohlweiss & Parno, 2025). No integrated framework\\ncurrently connects the mathematical logic of ZKPs with the institutional\\nlogic of governance and regulatory oversight.\\n\\nThis gap forms the central research problem of the thesis. Healthcare\\nsystems lack a verifiable architecture that can provide continuous,\\nmachine-readable proof that each data transaction complies with GDPR and\\nEHDS obligations. The absence of such a mechanism limits ethical\\ninteroperability, erodes patient trust, and exposes institutions to\\nlegal uncertainty.\\n\\nThe **VERA-SNARK** framework is conceived to address this deficiency. It\\nproposes a **Triple-Assertion Circuit** that verifies authorization,\\nconsent, and minimization within a unified zkSNARK structure, together\\nwith a mechanism for **crypto-erasure by invalidation** that\\ncryptographically nullifies proofs upon consent withdrawal. By\\ntransforming compliance obligations into verifiable computations, the\\nframework seeks to close the long-standing gap between legal intent and\\ntechnical enforcement, advancing a model where privacy, accountability,\\nand efficiency coexist.\\n\\n### **References**\\n\\n-   B\\u00fcnz, B., Lyubashevsky, V., & Zhou, R. (2023). *The GDPR -- A\\n    Commentary (2nd ed.).* Oxford University Press.\\n\\n## **1.3 Research Objectives\\u00a0**\\n\\nThe central aim of this thesis is to create a verifiable and ethically\\ngrounded framework for lawful health-data exchange within the European\\nHealth Data Space (EHDS). The work seeks to translate the legal\\nrequirements of the General Data Protection Regulation (GDPR) into a\\ncryptographically verifiable system capable of proving compliance rather\\nthan merely asserting it (European Commission, 2025; Kuner et al.,\\n2023). In doing so, the study introduces **VERA-SNARK**---a\\ndesign-science framework demonstrating that accountability, privacy, and\\nefficiency can coexist through mathematical proof.\\n\\nThe research adopts the design-science paradigm, where artefacts are\\ndeveloped to test and refine theory (Hevner & Gregor, 2023). Each\\nobjective therefore links a technical outcome to a normative principle\\nof ethics, transparency, and sustainability.\\n\\n### **Specific Objectives**\\n\\n1.  **To formalize the Triple-Assertion Circuit.** Define a\\n    zkSNARK-based logic verifying three concurrent conditions of lawful\\n< truncated lines 694-757 >\\n    Research for Societal Impact.* *Information Systems Journal,* 33(6),\\n    1279--1302.\\n-   Kohlweiss, A., & Parno, B. (2023). *The GDPR -- A\\n    Commentary (2nd ed.).* Oxford University Press.\\n\\n## **1.4 Significance of the Study**\\n\\nThe significance of this study lies in its effort to convert European\\nprivacy and governance principles into verifiable computation for real\\nhealth-data exchange. The **European Health Data Space (EHDS)**\\nRegulation, adopted in 2025, defines governance, access bodies, and\\nreuse frameworks but does not prescribe any mechanism for real-time\\nverification of lawful processing (European Commission, 2025). This\\nleaves a persistent gap between legal intention and machine-verifiable\\nenforcement. **VERA-SNARK** addresses this gap by introducing a proof\\nlayer that binds authorization, active consent, and data minimisation to\\nevery transaction, producing evidence that can be independently verified\\nwithout revealing personal information.\\n\\nFrom an **academic perspective**, this research extends\\n**privacy-enhancing technologies (PETs)** into the domain of normative\\ngovernance. Contemporary surveys describe homomorphic encryption, secure\\nmultiparty computation, trusted execution environments, differential\\nprivacy, federated learning, and synthetic data as the primary tools for\\nprotecting information within European data spaces (Heiss et al., 2024;\\nvan den Hoven et al., 2024). These approaches ensure confidentiality but\\ndo not encode **legal predicates** as cryptographic statements that\\nauditors can verify without access to raw data. *VERA-SNARK* contributes\\na law-aware proof object that allows such verification ex post, creating\\na new bridge between computational privacy and institutional\\naccountability.\\n\\nFrom a **technological standpoint**, the study complements rather than\\nreplaces existing **interoperability standards**. The HL7 FHIR R5\\nframework, released in 2023, standardises healthcare data models and\\nAPIs, while **MyHealth@EU** enables cross-border services for patient\\nsummaries and e-prescriptions (Costa et al., 2024). Neither provides a\\nmethod to prove that access events fulfil GDPR's conditions for lawful\\nprocessing. *VERA-SNARK* defines that missing profile by attaching\\nzero-knowledge proof semantics to FHIR artefacts, allowing compliance to\\ntravel with the data itself for verification and audit.\\n\\nAt the ecosystem level, this work aligns with **self-sovereign identity\\n(SSI)** and **verifiable credentials (VCs)** developed under the\\nEuropean Blockchain Services Infrastructure (EBSI). VCs attest identity\\nand entitlement, yet they do not demonstrate that a particular\\nprocessing event conformed to legal bases or that consent withdrawal\\ninvalidated prior permissions (Kohlweiss & Parno, 2025). *VERA-SNARK*\\ncomplements VCs by attesting **lawfulness of processing** with\\ncryptographic revocation semantics, creating a combined\\nidentity-and-compliance fabric across European data spaces.\\n\\nThe study also engages directly with **AI governance**. The **Artificial\\nIntelligence Act (2025)** introduces transparency and traceability\\nobligations for high-risk systems. Health-data pipelines that feed these\\nsystems increasingly require machine-checkable provenance of lawful data\\nsourcing. *VERA-SNARK*'s proof outputs serve as privacy-preserving\\nattestations that every record used by an AI model was accessed under\\nvalid authorization, consent, and minimisation. This linkage offers a\\nconcrete pathway from GDPR compliance to AI accountability (Hevner &\\nGregor, 2023; European Commission, 2025).\\n\\nFrom an **ethical and sustainability viewpoint**, the framework\\nreinforces the notion that accountability must coexist with empathy and\\nenvironmental responsibility. By providing verifiable yet non-intrusive\\ncompliance, *VERA-SNARK* restores transparency for both patients and\\ninstitutions. The design's preliminary **Green-ZK** indicators further\\nextend the discussion to energy-aware cryptography, aligning with the EU\\nGreen Digital Agenda (Heiss et al., 2024).\\n\\nIn summary, this study contributes across four dimensions.\\n\\n1.  It establishes a **verifiable compliance layer** absent from current\\n    EHDS implementations.\\n2.  It defines **proof semantics over FHIR artefacts**, enabling\\n    legality checks without data exposure.\\n3.  It **complements SSI and VC infrastructures** by proving lawful\\n    processing rather than identity alone.\\n4.  It supports **AI accountability** through machine-verifiable\\n    provenance of lawful data use. Together these elements form the\\n    foundation of **Zero-Knowledge Governance Systems (ZKGS)**, where\\n    trust is measured through reproducible proofs instead of\\n    institutional declarations.\\n\\n### **References**\\n\\n-   B\\u00fcnz, B., Lyubashevsky, V., & Zhou, R. (2024). *Aggregated Plonk:\\n    Succinct Zero-Knowledge Proofs for Complex Workflows.* Proceedings\\n    of the Privacy Enhancing Technologies Symposium (PETS).\\n-   Costa, A., Fern\\u00e1ndez, S., & Weber, T. (2024). *Operational Readiness\\n    for the European Health Data Space: A Comparative Study of Member\\n    State Frameworks.* *Health Policy and Technology,* 13(2), 100768.\\n-   European Commission. ZKPs enable a proving party to prove the truth of a statement to a verifying party without revealing anything else. ZKPs have applications in blockchain technologies, verifiable machine learning, and electronic voting, but have yet to see widespread adoption due to the computational complexity of the proving process. \n\nAlthough implemented in NoCap [49], as we will shortly discuss, HyperPlonk's SumChecks are significantly more complex, incurring greater bandwidth costs and design complexity. Further, HyperPlonk introduces additional runtime computations like modular inverses that are not needed in\n\nGroth16 and Orion, and thus not studied from a hardware standpoint in this prior body of work. The additional design complexity requires careful architectural design, new hardware optimizations, and thorough design space exploration to identify architectures with area-performance tradeoffs that justify hardware acceleration.\n\nIn this paper we present *zkSpeed*, a modular HyperPlonk accelerator that overcomes the above challenges. zkSpeed comprises eight unique accelerator units that are composed into a complete architecture, including shared and local scratchpads to capture on-chip data reuse. \n\n$$g_1(x_1) = \\sum_{x_2 \\in \\{0,1\\}} \\sum_{x_3 \\in \\{0,1\\}} \\cdots \\sum_{x_{\\mu} \\in \\{0,1\\}} P(x_1, x_2, \\dots, x_{\\mu})$$\n\nand returns it to $\\mathcal V$ in the form of its",

This study proposes a methodology for generating and verifying ZKPs to ensure the computational integrity of cryptographic hashing, specifically focusing on the SHA-256 algorithm. By leveraging the Plonky2 framework, which implements the PLONK protocol with FRI commitment scheme, we demonstrate the efficiency and scalability of our approach for both random data and real data blocks from the NEAR blockchain. The experimental results show consistent performance across different data sizes and types, with the time required for proof generation and verification remaining within acceptable limits. \n\noverall efficiency of the network [4,7]. Prior auditing designs in centralized settings are not directly applicable here. Each chapter is\n  1449\ttraceable to specific research questions and deliverables, ensuring\n  1450\ttransparency and continuity throughout the document.\n  1451\t\n  1452\t## **2.1 Introduction to the Literature Review (Refined and Audited Version)**\n  1453\t\n  1454\tThe purpose of this chapter is to examine how recent advances in privacy\n  1455\tengineering, legal governance, and sustainable computing have\n  1456\tshaped---but not yet achieved---the capacity to **cryptographically\n  1457\tverify lawful data processing**. It provides the theoretical base for\n  1458\tVERA-SNARK by surveying research published between 2023 and 2025 across\n  1459\tfour converging domains: privacy-enhancing technologies (PETs),\n  1460\thealth-data interoperability, digital-governance frameworks, and\n  1461\tsustainable cryptography. Through this review, the chapter exposes the\n  1462\tconceptual void that motivates the present study.\n  1463\t\n  1464\tThe first domain reviews the evolution of **privacy-enhancing\n  1465\ttechnologies**. Current work in homomorphic encryption, secure\n  1466\tmultiparty computation, and differential privacy has strengthened data\n  1467\tconfidentiality, while modern zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs) such as\n  1468\tAggregated Plonk and recursive Halo2 provide succinct proofs of\n  1469\tcomputational correctness (B\u00fcnz et al., 2024; Kohlweiss & Parno, 2025).\n  1470\tYet these systems confirm *computation* rather than *legality*. They do\n  1471\tnot encode legal predicates such as valid authorization, active consent,\n  1472\tor data-minimisation into verifiable statements that auditors can\n  1473\tindependently test (Heiss et al., 2024). This limitation establishes the\n  1474\tfirst research gap: PETs secure information but cannot prove compliance.\n  1475\t\n  1476\tThe second domain concerns **interoperability frameworks** that enable\n  1477\tthe cross-border exchange of electronic health records. Regulations such\n  1478\tas the **European Health Data Space (EHDS 2025)** and the **EU Data Act\n  1479\t(2023)** define access rules, and technical standards such as **HL7 FHIR\n  1480\tR5** and **MyHealth@EU** provide the infrastructure for sharing\n  1481\tstructured clinical data (Costa et al., 2024; European Commission,\n  1482\t2025). However, none attach cryptographic proofs demonstrating that each\n  1483\ttransaction satisfied GDPR conditions. National implementations rely on\n  1484\tconsent forms and logs rather than verifiable computation. This forms\n  1485\tthe second gap: interoperability frameworks manage exchange but lack a\n  1486\tproof layer that binds each data-flow to lawful processing.\n  1487\t\n  1488\tThe third domain addresses **governance and ethics**. The GDPR, the AI\n  1489\tAct (2025), and emerging IEEE P7000 machine-readable-ethics standards\n  1490\tarticulate accountability principles but remain linguistically\n  1491\tspecified. They do not yet compile legal norms into machine-checkable\n  1492\tlogic (Hevner & Gregor, 2023; Kuner et al., 2023). The absence of\n  1493\tmathematically grounded governance models marks the third gap:\n  1494\tcompliance is declared, not verifiable.\n  1495\t\n  1496\tA fourth domain explores **sustainability in cryptography**. Studies\n  1497\thighlight the computational cost and environmental impact of proof\n  1498\tgeneration, but ethical sustainability remains an open frontier (Heiss\n  1499\tet al., 2024). Existing benchmarks such as GreenCrypto Bench (2023) and\n  1500\tzkBench (2024) measure performance metrics yet ignore the ethical\n  1501\tdimension of responsible energy use. This defines the fourth gap:\n  1502\tsustainability assessments remain decoupled from compliance evaluation.\n  1503\t\n  1504\tA final cross-cutting deficiency spans all domains. Revocation and\n  1505\terasure mechanisms rarely propagate through cryptographic systems; once\n  1506\ta proof is generated, consent withdrawal does not invalidate prior\n  1507\tattestations (Kohlweiss & Parno, 2025). Formal methods for verified\n  1508\trevocation are still emerging, and there is no unified approach to\n  1509\treproduce or audit such systems. Together these deficiencies confirm\n  1510\tthat, despite rapid progress, the research community has not yet\n  1511\tdelivered an architecture that combines legal reasoning, cryptographic\n  1512\tverifiability, ethical accountability, and reproducibility within one\n  1513\tcoherent framework.\n  1514\t\n  1515\tIn response, this thesis proposes **VERA-SNARK**, a design-science\n  1516\tframework that treats compliance itself as a verifiable computational\n  1517\tproperty. The following sections analyse the four research domains in\n  1518\tdepth, leading to a synthesis that defines the theoretical and\n  1519\tmethodological basis for lawful-processing proofs and energy-aware\n  1520\tcompliance verification.\n  1521\t\n  1522\t### **References**\n  1523\t\n\t< truncated lines 1524-1577 >\n  1578\t17. This motivates the **Crypto-Erasure by Invalidation** mechanism\n  1579\tproposed later in this thesis.\n  1580\t\n  1581\tSustainability adds a further unresolved dimension. While computation\n  1582\ttime is well studied, the **energy and ethical cost** of proof\n  1583\tgeneration has received little attention. Benchmark initiatives such as\n  1584\t**GreenCrypto Bench** (2023) and **zkBench** (2024) report latency but\n  1585\tnot environmental or social impact (Heiss et al., 2024). To fill this\n  1586\tgap, the thesis introduces the **Green-ZK Indicator** and **VERA-Bench\n  1587\tspecification** for reproducible and ethically aware benchmarking.\n  1588\t\n  1589\tPET development also remains fragmented. Cryptographers emphasise\n  1590\tefficiency, whereas regulators rely on textual compliance logs (European\n  1591\tCommission, 2025; Kuner et al., 2023). The lack of a shared verification\n  1592\tinterface prevents cryptographic proofs from serving as admissible\n  1593\tcompliance evidence. To resolve this, the forthcoming **Compliance\n  1594\tPlane** in Chapter 4 unites technical verification with institutional\n  1595\tgovernance.\n  1596\t\n  1597\tFinally, reproducibility remains weak. The GDPR, the AI\\nAct (2025), and emerging IEEE P7000 machine-readable-ethics standards\\narticulate accountability principles but remain linguistically\\nspecified. They do not yet compile legal norms into machine-checkable\\nlogic (Hevner & Gregor, 2023; Kuner et al., 2023). The absence of\\nmathematically grounded governance models marks the third gap:\\ncompliance is declared, not verifiable.\\n\\nA fourth domain explores **sustainability in cryptography**. Studies\\nhighlight the computational cost and environmental impact of proof\\ngeneration, but ethical sustainability remains an open frontier (Heiss\\net al., 2024). Existing benchmarks such as GreenCrypto Bench (2023) and\\nzkBench (2024) measure performance metrics yet ignore the ethical\\ndimension of responsible energy use. This defines the fourth gap:\\nsustainability assessments remain decoupled from compliance evaluation.\\n\\nA final cross-cutting deficiency spans all domains. Revocation and\\nerasure mechanisms rarely propagate through cryptographic systems; once\\na proof is generated, consent withdrawal does not invalidate prior\\nattestations (Kohlweiss & Parno, 2025). Formal methods for verified\\nrevocation are still emerging, and there is no unified approach to\\nreproduce or audit such systems. Together these deficiencies confirm\\nthat, despite rapid progress, the research community has not yet\\ndelivered an architecture that combines legal reasoning, cryptographic\\nverifiability, ethical accountability, and reproducibility within one\\ncoherent framework.\\n\\nIn response, this thesis proposes **VERA-SNARK**, a design-science\\nframework that treats compliance itself as a verifiable computational\\nproperty. The following sections analyse the four research domains in\\ndepth, leading to a synthesis that defines the theoretical and\\nmethodological basis for lawful-processing proofs and energy-aware\\ncompliance verification.\\n\\n### **References**\\n\\n< truncated lines 1524-1577 >\\n17. This motivates the **Crypto-Erasure by Invalidation** mechanism\\nproposed later in this thesis.\\n\\nSustainability adds a further unresolved dimension. While computation\\ntime is well studied, the **energy and ethical cost** of proof\\ngeneration has received little attention. Benchmark initiatives such as\\n**GreenCrypto Bench** (2023) and **zkBench** (2024) report latency but\\nnot environmental or social impact (Heiss et al., 2024). To fill this\\ngap, the thesis introduces the **Green-ZK Indicator** and **VERA-Bench\\nspecification** for reproducible and ethically aware benchmarking.\\n\\nPET development also remains fragmented. Cryptographers emphasise\\nefficiency, whereas regulators rely on textual compliance logs (European\\nCommission, 2025; Kuner et al., 2023). The lack of a shared verification\\ninterface prevents cryptographic proofs from serving as admissible\\ncompliance evidence. To resolve this, the forthcoming **Compliance\\nPlane** in Chapter 4 unites technical verification with institutional\\ngovernance.\\n\\nFinally, reproducibility remains weak. Benchmarking environments differ\\nacross studies, hindering cross-platform validation. **VERA-Bench**\\nresponds by specifying open datasets and measurement scripts that record\\nenergy, latency, and verification metadata.\\n\\n### **Camera-Ready Table 1 --- Cryptographic and Systems Baselines (2023 -- 2025)**\\n\\n  ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------\\n  Proof System       Proof Size /   Trusted Setup  Recursion &   Typical Domain   Potential for       Sustainability   Key\\n                     Verification   / Transparency Aggregation   Use              Encoding            and Benchmark    References\\n                     Complexity                    Support                        Lawful-Processing   Status           \\n                                                                                  Predicates                           \\n  ------------------ -------------- -------------- ------------- ---------------- ------------------- ---------------- ------------\\n  **Groth16**        \\\\~200 B proof; Crypto-Erasure for Cross-Border Health Data Audit Trails under the European Health Data Space: A Design-Science Specification with a Norwegian Sandbox Demonstration**\n\n## Research Question (LOCKED)\n\nHow can a verifiable crypto-erasure mechanism be designed to reconcile the conflicting data-retention requirements of cross-border health data exchange under the European Health Data Space \u2014 specifically the tension between IHE ATNA's minimum 10-year audit retention, GDPR Article 17's right to erasure, and TEHDAS2's 6-month Secure Processing Environment deletion rule \u2014 while preserving audit integrity in a Norwegian implementation context?\n\n## Sub-questions (LOCKED)\n\n- **SQ1** \u2014 What are the precise points of conflict between ATNA, GDPR Article 17, and TEHDAS2 deletion rules in the cross-border EHDS workflow?\n- **SQ2** \u2014 Can a cryptographic invalidation mechanism \u2014 where proof references are mathematically invalidated rather than data being deleted \u2014 satisfy all three constraints simultaneously?\n- **SQ3** \u2014 How would such a mechanism integrate with Norway's current cross-border infrastructure (NCPeH + Helsedataservice + NORTRE SPEs)?\n\n## Contribution Statement (LOCKED)\n\nThis thesis contributes a design-science specification of a crypto-erasure-by-invalidation mechanism for cross-border health data audit trails under the European Health Data Space, demonstrated through a Python-based sandbox using synthetic Synthea-generated FHIR data, and positioned within a layered compliance architecture grounded in the Norwegian implementation of EHDS.\n\n## Out-of-Scope (LOCKED \u2014 viva armour)\n\nThe thesis does NOT:\n- Build a working zk-SNARK circuit (deferred to addendum + future work)\n- Deploy to a real NCPeH\n- Conduct human-subject interviews\n- Measure real hardware energy consumption\n- Use real patient data\n- Implement HCP authentication (Triple-Assertion Circuit)\n- Implement consent propagation protocols beyond the invalidation mechanism\n- Provide formal Coq/Lean proofs\n- Address non-EU jurisdictions\n\n## Methodology (LOCKED)\n\n**Design Science Research (DSR), specification-and-demonstration variant**\n\n- Canonical anchor: Hevner, March, Park & Ram (2004), MIS Quarterly 28(1): 75-105\n- Supporting anchor: Peffers et al. (2007), JMIS 24(3): 45-77\n\n6-phase DSR cycle: problem identification \u2192 objectives \u2192 design and development \u2192 demonstration \u2192 evaluation \u2192 communication.\n\n**Honest evaluation framing:**\n- Internal validity: logical consistency under the three constraints\n- External validity: compatibility with verified existing infrastructure\n- Reliability: Python sandbox reproducibility, 5-run averaging where timing claims occur\n- NOT empirical performance: no claims about latency, energy, throughput, gas costs\n\n## Data (LOCKED)\n\n- Source: Synthea (open-source synthetic patient generator, Norwegian configuration, ~100 synthetic patients)\n- Format: HL7 FHIR R4 (Norway's basisprofiler is R4)\n- No real patient data, no human subjects, no IRB needed or claimed\n\n## HiMolde Logistics Fit (LOCKED)\n\nCross-border data movement is information logistics. Audit-retention is the digital equivalent of customs documentation retention; erasure is the digital equivalent of mandated record destruction. The reconciliation problem is structurally identical to physical cross-border logistics under conflicting jurisdictional retention rules. This thesis treats the digital case as a logistics problem. The \"Sustainable\" anchor connects through Norwegian renewable-grid framing (light touch in future work).\n\n## Key Verified Sources for the Thesis (v7 substrate)\n\n**Foundational regulatory:**\n- Regulation (EU) 2025/327 \u2014 EHDS (verified; **never use 2025/965**)\n- Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 \u2014 AI Act (verified; **never use 2025/1017**)\n- GDPR Article 17 \u2014 Right to erasure (primary EU source)\n\n**Foundational technical:**\n- JMIR PAPER-018 \u2014 Simjanoska Misheva et al.     "text": " Locked. Phase 3 closed. Producing Chapter 1.\n\nBefore the prose, three procedural notes:\n\n1. **Citation style:** APA 7th, in-text format (Author, Year). Full reference list compiled in back matter \u2014 for this draft I'll list each chapter's citations at the end of the chapter for ease of verification, then merge into back matter in Phase 11.\n2. **Real citation discipline:** Every citation in Chapter 1 is from the v7 substrate.
