Where a Norwegian primary source has no traditional author, I cite the issuing body (Helsedirektoratet, etc.) per APA 7th rules for corporate authors.\n3. **Word counts:** Section-by-section budgets respected. Total target 2,400 words.\n\n---\n\n# \ud83d\udcdd CHAPTER 1 \u2014 INTRODUCTION (draft v1)\n\n---\n\n## 1.1 Background and Context\n\n(target: 600 words)\n\nThe European Union's health sector is undergoing the most significant data-governance transformation in its history. In March 2025, the European Health Data Space Regulation (EU) 2025/327 entered into force, establishing the first Union-wide legal framework for the primary and secondary use of electronic health data across all Member States and European Economic Area countries (European Parliament and Council of the European Union, 2025). The regulation creates a binding architecture for cross-border exchange of patient summaries, electronic prescriptions, laboratory results, medical imaging, and hospital discharge reports, with primary-use exchange of patient summaries and ePrescriptions becoming mandatory across the EU and EEA by March 2029, and the remaining priority categories by March 2031 (European Commission, 2025a).\n\nThe technical infrastructure underpinning this regulatory shift is MyHealth@EU, the federated pull-based system that allows a clinician in one Member State to retrieve a patient summary or dispense an ePrescription issued in another Member State. Each participating country operates a single National Contact Point for eHealth (NCPeH), and all cross-border exchanges flow through a six-step gateway protocol implemented in the Open National Contact Point (OpenNCP) software stack. These six steps cover service discovery, patient discovery, document retrieval, pivot translation between national clinical document formats and a common European format, security and consent validation, and audit logging (Simjanoska Misheva et al., 2025).\n\nNorway is in the late stages of operational integration with MyHealth@EU. The Norwegian connection project began in January 2023 under joint coordination between the Norwegian Directorate of Health (Helsedirektoratet) and the Norwegian Health Network (Norsk Helsenett SF), with go-live piloting scheduled for 2025 alongside Austria, Cyprus, Denmark, Hungary, Iceland, Italy, Romania, Slovenia, and Sweden (European Commission, 2025b). As Norway is not a Member State of the European Union but an EEA member, EHDS implementation is being processed through the EEA Joint Committee, with the Norwegian Directorate of Health having delivered its formal EHDS gap analysis on 11 April 2025 (Helsedirektoratet, 2025a).\n\nWithin this regulatory transformation, the most consequential change for clinical practice is the new layer of patient rights established by EHDS. Citizens gain the right to access and download their electronic health data, to view full logs of who has accessed it, to set granular restrictions on cross-border sharing, and to opt out of secondary use (European Parliament and Council of the European Union, 2025). These rights interact directly with two long-standing technical standards. The first is IHE Audit Trail and Node Authentication (ATNA), the integration profile that defines how access events are logged in cross-border exchanges. Under the eHDSI specification adopted for MyHealth@EU, ATNA-compliant audit repositories must retain logs for a minimum of ten years (Simjanoska Misheva et al., 2025). The second is the General Data Protection Regulation (Regulation (EU) 2016/679), whose Article 17 establishes the data subject's right to obtain erasure of personal data without undue delay under specified conditions (European Parliament and Council of the European Union, 2016).\n\nA third constraint is now emerging from the implementing infrastructure for EHDS secondary use. The draft Guideline for Health Data Access Bodies on procedures and formats for data access, published by the TEHDAS2 Joint Action in September 2025, specifies that personal data must be deleted from a Secure Processing Environment within six months of the expiry of the data permit, under Article 68(12) of the EHDS Regulation (TEHDAS2, 2025).\n\nCross-border health data exchange therefore operates under three distinct retention regimes that do not yet have a documented technical reconciliation: a ten-year retention floor for audit logs, an unbounded erasure obligation for personal data on subject request, and a six-month deletion ceiling for processed data in Secure Processing Environments. This thesis is positioned within that unresolved tension.\n\n---\n\n## 1.2 Problem Statement\n\n(target: 500 words)\n\nThe European Health Data Space establishes legal rights and operational obligations, but it does not yet prescribe how the three retention regimes described above are to be reconciled in technical practice. The result is a structural conflict embedded in the cross-border audit trail.\n\nWhen a Norwegian patient is treated in another Member State, the access event is logged in the local NCPeH's ATNA-compliant audit repository, retained for at least ten years, and accessible to the patient through the home country's audit-log interface (Simjanoska Misheva et al., 2025). If, during that ten-year window, the patient exercises the right to erasure under GDPR Article 17, the institution faces a choice between three positions, each of which violates one of the other regimes. Deleting the audit record honours Article 17 but destroys the integrity of the audit chain that ATNA, eIDAS-based access traceability, and EHDS patient rights all depend upon. Retaining the audit record preserves the chain but fails the erasure obligation. Pseudonymising the record creates ambiguity about whether the pseudonymisation is reversible, which under GDPR Recital 26 may not satisfy true anonymisation.\n\nThe same conflict arises in the secondary-use pathway. When a researcher receives a data permit from the Norwegian Health Data Access Body, Helsedataservice (Folkehelseinstituttet, 2024), and processes the data within a Norwegian Secure Processing Environment such as those operated by the Trusted Research Environments collaboration NORTRE (Norwegian Trusted Research Environments, 2025), the TEHDAS2 specification requires deletion of personal data from the SPE within six months of permit expiry. Yet audit records of the access, processing, and analytical operations performed inside the SPE remain subject to the ATNA ten-year minimum.\n\nThese conflicts are not theoretical. The Norwegian SPUHiN project, jointly led by Helsedirektoratet and the Norwegian Institute of Public Health, explicitly acknowledged in its November 2024 draft Recommendations for Minimum Requirements for Secure Processing Environments in Norway that the country lacks a formal regulatory definition of what constitutes a closed and secure analysis infrastructure, and that the definitional work itself remains contested (SPUHiN, 2024). Norwegian implementation of the EHDS therefore proceeds against a regulatory and technical background in which the audit-retention versus right-to-erasure question is not yet resolved.\n\nA small but rapidly growing body of cryptographic research has begun to address adjacent problems. Albalwy (2026) demonstrates zero-knowledge policy enforcement for cross-institutional health data sharing within a single jurisdiction. Natsiavas et al. (2021) document KONFIDO, the most direct architectural ancestor for cross-border health data security, which combined trusted execution environments, homomorphic encryption, and blockchain-based auditing on the OpenNCP gateway. Bai et al. (2022) describe Health-zkIDM, a Hyperledger Fabric-based decentralised identity system for healthcare. None of these works addresses the audit-retention versus erasure tension at the cross-border audit layer.\n\nThe problem this thesis addresses is therefore the absence of a technically specified, regulation-aligned mechanism for reconciling the three retention regimes of cross-border health data audit trails under EHDS, in a manner compatible with the operational reality of Norwegian implementation.\n\n---\n\n## 1.3 Research Objectives and Questions\n\n(target: 350 words)\n\nThe objective of this thesis is to specify and demonstrate a verifiable mechanism that reconciles the conflicting retention regimes identified in Section 1.2. The mechanism is termed Crypto-Erasure by Invalidation. Its design rests on a single conceptual move: rather than deleting audit records to satisfy GDPR Article 17, the cryptographic references that bind the audit record to retrievable personal data are mathematically invalidated, so that the audit chain remains intact for the ATNA ten-year minimum and for accountability purposes, but the personal data referenced from the audit record can no longer be reconstructed.\n\nThe thesis is guided by one primary research question.\n\n**Primary research question.** How can a verifiable crypto-erasure mechanism be designed to reconcile the conflicting data-retention requirements of cross-border health data exchange under the European Health Data Space \u2014 specifically the tension between IHE ATNA's minimum ten-year audit retention, GDPR Article 17's right to erasure, and TEHDAS2's six-month Secure Processing Environment deletion rule \u2014 while preserving audit integrity in a Norwegian implementation context?\n\nThe primary question is decomposed into three sub-questions.\n\n**SQ1.** What are the precise points of conflict between ATNA, GDPR Article 17, and TEHDAS2 deletion rules in the cross-border EHDS workflow?\n\n**SQ2.** Can a cryptographic invalidation mechanism \u2014 where proof references are mathematically invalidated rather than data being deleted \u2014 satisfy all three constraints simultaneously?\n\n**SQ3.** How would such a mechanism integrate with Norway's current cross-border infrastructure, specifically the NCPeH operated by Helsedirektoratet and Norsk Helsenett, the Health Data Access Body Helsedataservice, and the Secure Processing Environments operated under NORTRE?\n\nThese three sub-questions correspond to the analytical, design, and integration dimensions of the contribution. SQ1 is addressed primarily through Chapter 2's literature review and Chapter 4's design rationale. SQ2 is addressed through Chapter 4's specification and Chapter 5's sandbox demonstration. SQ3 is addressed through Chapter 4's integration analysis and Chapter 6's discussion of practical implications for Norwegian implementing actors.\n\n---\n\n## 1.4 Significance and Relevance\n\n(target: 400 words)\n\nThe significance of this thesis rests on three convergent factors that make the audit-retention versus erasure question both timely and concretely Norwegian.\n\nFirst, the timing is not abstract. The Group One priority categories of EHDS, comprising patient summaries and ePrescriptions, become mandatory across the EU and EEA in March 2029, with the Group Two categories of laboratory results, medical imaging, and hospital discharge reports following in March 2031 (European Commission, 2025a). The implementing acts that will specify technical detail are due by March 2027. Norway's NCPeH is in late-stage piloting, with operational rollout scheduled across 2025 and 2026 (European Commission, 2025b). Decisions about how audit trails will handle conflicting retention obligations will be made within the next twenty-four months, in a regulatory environment that does not yet have a settled answer.\n\nSecond, the Norwegian implementation context provides a concrete substrate. Norway has a defined Health Data Access Body in Helsedataservice (Folkehelseinstituttet, 2024); a developed Trusted Research Environment collaboration in NORTRE (Norwegian Trusted Research Environments, 2025); an active national policy consultation through the SPUHiN project (SPUHiN, 2024); and parallel ongoing legislative work on Pasientjournalloven and Helsepersonelloven covering professional confidentiality and cross-organisational access (Stortinget, 2025). The thesis can therefore be positioned against a real, named, currently active implementation pathway rather than against a hypothetical European deployment.\n\nThird, the novelty positioning is defensible against current peer-reviewed literature. Albalwy (2026) addresses zero-knowledge policy enforcement for cross-institutional health data sharing within a single jurisdiction, occupying the cross-institutional space distinct from the cross-border space this thesis addresses. KONFIDO (Natsiavas et al., 2021) addressed cross-border security but did not resolve the audit-retention versus erasure tension and was hardware-dependent through its use of trusted execution environments. The 2025 research agenda for privacy-enhancing technologies under EHDS (Frontiers in Digital Health, 2025) identifies governance and technological solutions as an open category but does not specify mechanisms for the specific tension this thesis addresses.\n\nThe thesis also frames the problem as one of information logistics. The reconciliation of conflicting retention regimes across jurisdictional boundaries is structurally identical to the challenges faced by cross-border logistics under conflicting record-keeping requirements imposed by different national authorities. This framing situates the contribution within the disciplinary identity of Sustainable Energy Logistics at HiMolde, treating digital health data as a flow problem under regulatory constraint.\n\n---\n\n## 1.5 Scope and Out-of-Scope\n\n(target: 350 words)\n\nThis thesis is bounded by a deliberate and explicit scope. The bounding is not a limitation to be apologised for but a methodological commitment to defensibility within the time and resources available for an MSc thesis.\n\n**The thesis contributes a design-science specification.** It specifies the mechanism, demonstrates it through a Python-based sandbox using synthetic Synthea-generated FHIR data, and positions it within a layered compliance architecture grounded in the Norwegian implementation of EHDS. The specification and the sandbox together constitute the artefact.\n\nThe thesis explicitly does **not** include the following. First, it does not build a working zero-knowledge SNARK circuit; the cryptographic primitive that would underpin a production implementation is treated as a deferrable engineering layer and is briefly discussed in Chapter 7 as future work. Second, it does not deploy to a real National Contact Point for eHealth; integration with Norwegian NCPeH operations remains a theoretical mapping rather than a runtime test. Third, it does not conduct human-subject interviews; no clinicians, compliance officers, or patients were interviewed for this thesis, and no qualitative empirical data are reported. Fourth, it does not measure real hardware energy consumption; no claims about energy or sustainability metrics are made beyond conceptual framing. Fifth, it does not use real patient data; all data used in the sandbox are synthetic, generated by Synthea, and contain no information about real individuals. Sixth, it does not implement healthcare professional authentication mechanisms or consent propagation protocols beyond the invalidation mechanism that is the subject of the thesis. Seventh, it does not provide formal Coq or Lean proofs of the cryptographic properties of the proposed mechanism; the security argument is presented informally under standard assumptions. Eighth, it does not address non-EU and non-EEA jurisdictions.\n\nThree components that are conceptually related to the broader research direction \u2014 a Triple-Assertion Circuit for healthcare professional authorisation, a sustainability indicator for zero-knowledge proof systems, and a cross-border consent revocation propagation protocol \u2014 are presented in a separate addendum to the thesis supervisor, signalling potential future research direction without claiming results that have not been produced.\n\n---\n\n## 1.6 Methodological Approach\n\n(target: 250 words)\n\nThe thesis adopts Design Science Research, as established by Hevner, March, Park, and Ram (2004) and elaborated by Peffers, Tuunanen, Rothenberger, and Chatterjee (2007). Design Science Research is the appropriate paradigm where a research project produces an artefact intended to address a defined problem in an information-systems context, evaluates the artefact, and communicates the contribution. The framework prescribes a cycle of problem identification, definition of objectives, design and development of the artefact, demonstration, evaluation, and communication.\n\nThis thesis adopts the specification-and-demonstration variant of Design Science Research. The artefact takes the form of a mechanism specification supported by a software demonstration. The demonstration is conducted in a controlled sandbox environment using synthetic data generated by Synthea, an open-source tool for the generation of synthetic patient records compliant with HL7 FHIR R4. The choice of FHIR R4 reflects the current Norwegian basisprofiler, which is the official Norwegian national constraint set on the international FHIR R4 standard, published and maintained by HL7 Norway (HL7 Norway, 2024).\n\nThe evaluation strategy is qualitative and analytical rather than quantitative and empirical. Internal validity is assessed through logical consistency under the three retention constraints. External validity is assessed through compatibility with verified existing infrastructure. Reliability is supported through deterministic sandbox execution with fixed random seeds. No empirical performance benchmarks are reported, and no claims about latency, throughput, energy consumption, or computational cost are presented as measurement results. The full methodological treatment is provided in Chapter 3.\n\n---\n\n## 1.7 Thesis Structure\n\n(target: 200 words)\n\nThe thesis is organised in seven chapters and two appendices.\n\n**Chapter 1** introduces the regulatory background, the problem, the research objectives, the significance of the study, the scope and explicit out-of-scope boundaries, and the methodological approach.\n\n**Chapter 2** reviews the literature in four domains: the cross-border health data exchange landscape under EHDS, the audit-retention versus right-to-erasure tension, cryptographic approaches to audit compliance, and the Norwegian secondary-use infrastructure. It identifies the conceptual gap that this thesis addresses and positions the contribution within the broader research agenda for privacy-enhancing technologies in European health data spaces.\n\n**Chapter 3** establishes the design science research paradigm, explains the choice of the specification-and-demonstration variant, describes the synthetic data methodology and the sandbox approach, and addresses validity, reliability, and ethical considerations.\n\n**Chapter 4** specifies the Crypto-Erasure by Invalidation mechanism, including design goals, conceptual model, architectural positioning, the invalidation registry, audit-trail behaviour, and integration analysis with Norwegian NCPeH and NORTRE infrastructure.\n\n**Chapter 5** presents the sandbox demonstration. **Chapter 6** discusses findings and their implications for practice, theory, and policy. **Chapter 7** concludes, summarises contributions, revisits limitations, and outlines future work.\n\n---\n\n## References (Chapter 1)\n\nAlbalwy, F. It allows Member States to offer a full opt-out from the data-sharing infrastructure established under the regulation. It also allows opt-out provisions for secondary use of data which offer flexibility while maintaining a balance between respecting patient preferences and ensuring that essential health data remains available for public interest purposes. To further strengthen security, the EHDS mandates secure processing environments that comply with strict privacy and cybersecurity safeguards. These measures are essential to protecting individuals\u2019 sensitive health data while enabling innovation and efficiency in healthcare. \n\nAs discussed, this regulation is a long-term endeavour, meaning its implementation will take place in phases. Healthcare professionals will have access to more comprehensive patient records, possibly leading to better-informed decisions, faster diagnoses, and more effective treatments.\n\nAs mentioned in our previous European Health Data Space (EHDS) blogpost back in March 2024, consensus was reached between the European Parliament and the Council of the EU concerning the EHDS.     "text": "Excellent. Three useful 2026-dated implementation perspectives surfaced (Healthy Europe, ictrecht.nl, mydata-trust). Each provides current-state framing. Now drafting Run 1.\n\n---\n\n# Chapter 2 \u2014 Literature Review and Theoretical Foundation\n\n## 2.1 Review Strategy\n\n(target 400 words)\n\nThe literature review in this chapter is structured around the conceptual architecture of the thesis rather than a comprehensive bibliographic survey. The aim is to establish, with verifiable sources, the four conditions on which the thesis contribution depends: that the cross-border health data landscape under the European Health Data Space is sufficiently advanced and Norwegian participation sufficiently committed for the contribution to be timely; that the audit-retention versus right-to-erasure tension is real, named in primary regulatory and technical sources, and not yet resolved; that cryptographic compliance approaches have advanced enough to make the proposed mechanism technically plausible while leaving the specific cross-border erasure problem unaddressed; and that the Norwegian secondary-use infrastructure provides a concrete substrate within which the contribution can be positioned.\n\nSources were identified through a combination of structured database searches, citation snowballing from foundational papers, and direct retrieval of primary regulatory, standards, and government documents. Peer-reviewed literature was searched in Scopus, PubMed, IEEE Xplore, ACM Digital Library, and Google Scholar, restricted to publications dated 2022 through May 2026, using combinations of the terms zero-knowledge, blockchain, health data, electronic health records, cross-border, EHDS, MyHealth@EU, GDPR Article 17, audit, erasure, crypto-shredding, and Secure Processing Environment. Primary regulatory sources, including Regulation (EU) 2025/327 establishing the European Health Data Space, the General Data Protection Regulation, and TEHDAS2 deliverables, were retrieved directly from their authoritative European Union or Joint Action repositories. Norwegian primary sources, including Helsedirektoratet's EHDS landing page, the SPUHiN minimum requirements draft, and the relevant amendments to Pasientjournalloven and Helsepersonelloven, were retrieved directly from helsedirektoratet.no, helsedata.no, and stortinget.no.\n\nTwo inclusion criteria were applied. First, every cited source must be retrievable through a public URL, a DOI, or a primary repository at the time of writing, so that examiners can independently verify any claim. Second, in cases of conflict between secondary commentary and primary regulatory text, the primary text prevails. Where secondary sources are cited, this is for analysis or interpretation rather than for the factual content of the regulation or standard itself.\n\nThe review proceeds through four substantive domains in Sections 2.2 to 2.5, develops the information-logistics framing that anchors the contribution within the Sustainable Energy Logistics discipline in Section 2.6, compares the proposed contribution against verified existing work in Section 2.7, identifies the research gap in Section 2.8, and positions the thesis contribution against that gap in Section 2.9.\n\n## 2.2 The Cross-Border Health Data Exchange Landscape\n\n(target 1,000 words)\n\nThe European Union's cross-border health data infrastructure rests on a regulatory framework that has matured rapidly during the past three years, and on a technical platform that has been in operation, in voluntary form, for more than a decade. The two layers are now converging under the European Health Data Space Regulation, which establishes the legal architecture within which the existing technical platform must scale.\n\nRegulation (EU) 2025/327 of 11 February 2025 establishing the European Health Data Space entered into force on 26 March 2025, opening a multi-year transition during which Member States and European Economic Area countries must align their national infrastructures with binding Union-wide requirements (European Parliament and Council of the European Union, 2025). The regulation governs both primary use, meaning the use of electronic health data for the provision of healthcare to the data subject, and secondary use, meaning use for research, innovation, policy-making, regulatory activities, and public health (European Commission, 2025a). For primary use, the regulation establishes a calendar that the implementing acts must operationalise by 26 March 2027, with Group One priority categories of patient summaries and ePrescriptions becoming exchangeable across all Member States by 26 March 2029, and Group Two categories of laboratory results, medical imaging, and hospital discharge reports following by 26 March 2031 (Healthy Europe, 2026; European Commission, 2025a). Each Member State must designate a National Digital Health Authority by June 2025, and healthcare providers and electronic health record vendors must certify their systems for interoperability and security compliance by January 2026 (Ernst & Young, 2025).\n\nThe technical platform implementing cross-border primary use is MyHealth@EU, the operational successor to the eHealth Digital Service Infrastructure (eHDSI). MyHealth@EU connects national systems through National Contact Points for eHealth (NCPeH), each operated by the participating country's competent authority and funded historically through the Connecting Europe Facility Telecom Programme (2015\u20132020) and currently through the EU4Health Programme (2021\u20132027) (European Commission, 2025b). Governance is exercised by two coordinating bodies: the eHealth DSI EU countries Expert Group (eHMSEG), composed of managers responsible for NCPeH implementation in participating countries, and the eHealth Operational Management Board (eHOMB), composed of European Commission representatives and the eHMSEG Chairs (European Commission, 2025b).\n\nThe MyHealth@EU operational architecture follows a six-step transmission flow at the NCPeH gateway level, recently characterised in detail by Simjanoska Misheva et al. (2025). When a healthcare provider in a country of treatment requests cross-border data, the source country NCPeH performs service discovery to locate the patient's home country gateway; performs patient discovery against the patient's national identifier; retrieves the requested document, typically an International Patient Summary or an ePrescription, in the source country's native clinical document format; transforms it through a pivot transformer that converts national clinical document representations to the common European Electronic Health Record Exchange Format using SNOMED CT, LOINC, and other terminology bindings; applies the security and consent module, which validates the requesting clinician's eIDAS identity token and applies XACML-based consent policies; and finally logs the transaction in an IHE Audit Trail and Node Authentication compliant audit repository with a minimum ten-year retention period.\n\nNorway's integration with MyHealth@EU is being implemented through a project that began in January 2023 under the joint operational responsibility of Helsedirektoratet and Norsk Helsenett SF, the national operator of the closed Norwegian health network. Norway is scheduled for go-live piloting in 2025 alongside Austria, Cyprus, Denmark, Hungary, Iceland, Italy, Romania, Slovenia, and Sweden (European Commission, 2025c). Because Norway is a member of the European Economic Area rather than of the European Union, the EHDS Regulation does not apply directly but is being processed for incorporation through the EEA Joint Committee. The Norwegian Directorate of Health delivered its formal EHDS impact and gap analysis on 11 April 2025, identifying the principal areas in which Norwegian law and infrastructure will require alignment (Helsedirektoratet, 2025a).\n\nParallel to the primary-use track, the regulation establishes a secondary-use track through which national Health Data Access Bodies coordinate access to electronic health data for research, innovation, and policy purposes. The interconnecting infrastructure for secondary use is HealthData@EU, which complements MyHealth@EU and operates under different technical and governance arrangements. The technical, functional, and security specifications for the Secure Processing Environments through which secondary-use data must be processed are being developed under the TEHDAS2 Joint Action, a coordinated effort of twenty-nine countries running until December 2026 (Healthy Europe, 2026). The Norwegian Health Data Access Body designated under this regime is Helsedataservice, hosted at the Folkehelseinstituttet (Norwegian Institute of Public Health), which since 2023 has held the decision-making authority for eleven statutory national health registries (Folkehelseinstituttet, 2024).\n\nThe historical context of these institutional arrangements is important for the present thesis. The Norwegian central analytics platform Helseanalyseplattformen was paused in December 2021 following legal challenges related to the Schrems II judgment on transatlantic data transfers, and was formally avviklet by the Ministry of Health and Care Services in February 2023. The Direktoratet for e-helse, which had led much of the early national e-health standards work, was itself dissolved in May 2023, with its core functions absorbed into Helsedirektoratet from 2024 onwards (Helsedirektoratet, 2023). The Norwegian secondary-use infrastructure that the EHDS will be implemented against is therefore not a centralised analytic platform but a distributed model resting on the three university-based trusted research environments \u2014 TSD at the University of Oslo, SAFE at the University of Bergen, and HUNT Cloud at NTNU \u2014 coordinated through the NORTRE collaboration (Norwegian Trusted Research Environments, 2025).\n\nThe cross-border landscape is therefore mature in its regulatory architecture, advancing in its technical infrastructure, and concretely defined in its Norwegian implementation. It is against this landscape that the audit-retention and erasure tensions become not abstract but operationally consequential, as Section 2.3 will demonstrate.\n\n---\n\n## References used in Run 1 (added to citation ledger)\n\nAll citations in this run are verified against v7 substrate. These maps provide detailed insights into the performance and effectiveness of health services across the European Union. The eHealth Digital Service Infrastructure (eHDSI) facilitates the cross-border exchange of health data including patient summaries and e-prescription. Through 'core services', the European Commission is providing a common ICT infrastructure and crosscutting services (terminology, interoperability etc.) to EU countries. \n\nThey can then set up 'generic services' to connect national eHealth systems through 'National Contact Points for eHealth (NCPeH)', with financial assistance from the Connecting Europe Facility Telecom Programme (2015-2020) and from the EU4Health Programme (2021-2027).\n\nThe eHealth Digital Service Infrastructure (eHDSI) is an infrastructure ensuring the continuity of care for European citizens while they are travelling abroad in the EU. This gives EU countries the possibility to exchange health data in a secure, efficient and interoperable way.\n\nThe eHealth Operational Management Board (eHOMB) is composed of representatives of internal services of the European Commission and the eHMSEG Chairs. The first main service is the dispensation of medicine by interlinking national electronic prescription systems. \n\nThe second one is the Patient Summary, which enables providing the basic set of patients\u2019 medical data. Methods: The contemporary technical documentation of the project was studied and selected published Key Performance Indicators of the project were analyzed. Where necessary, data were acquired directly from the European Commission. Results: Data from the start of the project (fourth quarter of 2019) until the second quarter of 2022 were analyzed. During this time both the overall number of EU countries with operational cross-border healthcare and their particular abilities in both services have risen. At present, there are eleven countries with capabilities in at least one of the services, of which nine have reported transactions. More countries are in the test phase now and will join the operational phase of the project shortly. This gives us a good reason to take a closer look at Article 17 GDPR. In accordance with their Coordinated Enforcement Framework (CEF), the data protection authorities select a prioritised and usually particularly practice-relevant topic for what they call their Coordinated Action each year. \n\nThe CEF aims to \"streamline\" and optimise the implementation of the GDPR and cooperation between national data protection supervisory authorities in the EU. The individual national authorities can join the Coordinated Action on a voluntary basis.\n\nThe following reasons for erasure are important from a practical perspective: Cessation of purpose, i.e. the data are no longer necessary in relation to the purposes for which they were collected or otherwise processed \u2013 Article 17 (1) (a) GDPR: This alternative exists, for example, if the data have been processed to establish or perform a contract and the contract has been fulfilled (exception: retention obligations, see below). Withdrawal of consent if there is no other legal basis for (further) processing \u2013 Article 17 (1) (b) GDPR: This alternative includes, for example, cases in which the data subject had given consent for a newsletter to be sent but has withdrawn this consent. The Board selected this topic as it is one of the most frequently exercised GDPR rights and one about which DPAs frequently receive complaints from individuals. The main objectives of this coordinated action are to ensure that the right to erasure is effectively exercised by individuals in Europe and understand how controllers comply with this right in practice. In addition, the EDPB identified good practices and the most important related challenges, with the aim of providing further guidance on this topic.         "text": "Healthcare Data: Medical data may be subject to erasure rights while balancing public health interests and legal retention obligations. Erasure rights interact with other GDPR individual rights and broader legal frameworks: Access Rights ...\n\nHealthcare Data: Medical data may be subject to erasure rights while balancing public health interests and legal retention obligations. Erasure rights interact with other GDPR individual rights and broader legal frameworks: Access Rights Integration: Individuals often exercise access rights before requesting erasure to understand what data requires deletion. Rectification Relationship: Erasure may be preferred over rectification when personal data cannot be accurately corrected. Restriction Alternative: Data processing restriction may serve as alternative to erasure when deletion is not technically feasible. Portability Coordination: Data portability and erasure rights may be exercised together during service transitions. \n\nObjection Rights: Successful objections to processing may lead to erasure requirements when processing cannot continue lawfully. GDPR Article 17 establishes specific grounds for erasure while balancing individual rights with legitimate interests in data retention and \n\nAs discussed in our Data Protection Officer guide, DPOs often oversee erasure right implementation and provide guidance on complex erasure scenarios. GDPR Article 17(3) establishes specific exceptions where erasure rights do not apply, balancing individual privacy with other fundamental rights and legitimate interests. The most significant exception protects freedom of expression and information rights: Journalistic Purposes: Media organizations may retain personal data necessary for journalistic activities and news reporting. Academic Expression: Academic freedom protections may override erasure rights for research and scholarly publications.",

Appendices B and C detail best practices that crypto shredding matches. California Consumer Privacy Act (CCPA): Following the logic of GDPR, CCPA requires businesses to respond to deletion requests from consumers. Crypto shredding provides provable, rapid data invalidation that supports compliance with Section 1798.105. In each case, regulatory frameworks recognize data as effectively destroyed when rendered inaccessible through cryptography \u2014 so long as key management practices meet defined security standards. \n\nLaws in jurisdictions like the EU under GDPR or the US under HIPAA often require demonstrable data erasure. Without explicit regulatory acknowledgement that key destruction equals data destruction, organizations face compliance gray zones.\n\nCrypto shredding offers a direct, scalable, and cost-efficient mechanism for rendering sensitive data inaccessible. By cryptographically erasing encryption keys rather than pursuing physical data destruction, organizations achieve irreversible deletion with far fewer resources. No residual data remains accessible, and the technique sidesteps the logistical complexities of traditional shredding or wiping. Regulatory compliance further solidifies its value.       {
        "type": "knowledge",
        "title": "What Does NIST SP 800-88r2 Say About Cryptographic Erase?",
        "url": "https://www.bitraser.com/blog/cryptographic-erase-and-supported-devices/",
        "metadata": {
          "type": "webpage_metadata",
          "site_domain": "bitraser.com",
          "favicon_url": "https://www.google.com/s2/favicons?sz=64&domain=bitraser.com",
          "site_name": "BitRaser"
        },
        "is_missing": false,
        "text": "Frequently Asked Questions Our Top FAQs That Will Help You Get Answers To Your Questions. Blogs Gain latest insights into data erasure, data protection, privacy and regulations. ... Summary: Cryptographic erase (CE) is the only technology-specific sanitization technique that has been mentioned in NIST SP 800 88r2 media sani\n\nUnder Section 3.2, Cryptographic Erase (CE) is a purge sanitization technique that sanitizes the key used to encrypt data or prevents access to this key. By erasing the key itself, access to the encrypted information is prevented, leaving the encrypted data, or ciphertext, on the ISM. Since CE performs key sanitization, it is comparatively faster than other sanitization techniques and has high assurance. Today, many ISMs have integrated symmetric-key encryption, which is always active and encrypts all the data stored on the ISM. One such ISM example is self-encrypting drives (SEDs) that usually include sanitization capabilities. \n\nFor successful implementation of cryptographic erasure, it is important for the organization to have a systematic process in place of recording the media devices that were encrypted using a strong cryptographic algorithm along with a log of encryption keys used to encrypt the media.\n\nEncryption keys must be managed securely, with access limited to authorized personnel only. When it comes to sanitizing storage media using the CE technique, it is important to use a professional data-wiping tool that supports cryptographic erasure as prescribed by NIST 800-88 R2. The software must be able to perform verification after CE to verify that the keys have been securely erased and the data is no longer accessible.     "type": "text",
    "text": "I have everything I need. Critical findings from this deep search round:\n\n1. **NIST SP 800-88 Rev.2** (current version) \u2014 primary US/global standard recognising cryptographic erasure as valid sanitization. This anchors my A4 mechanism in established standards literature, **not** as a novel invention. This is a major reframe.\n2. **EDPB CEF Report on Right to Erasure** (18 Feb 2026) \u2014 32 DPAs, 764 controllers, focused on Article 17 implementation. Explicitly identifies Issue 5: \"Difficulties in defining and implementing data retention periods\" \u2014 **directly supports the thesis problem statement.**\n3. **Article 17(3)(b) \u2014 legal obligation exemption** and **Article 17(3)(d) \u2014 research exemption (EDPB 2026 Guidelines)** \u2014 the precise legal mechanisms by which an organisation can lawfully refuse to delete data referenced in audit trails.\n4. **SecuPi's dissenting view** (June 2025) \u2014 \"Crypto-Shredding is NOT Nirvana for RTBF Compliance\" \u2014 gives me a defensible counter-position to address in Chapter 6 discussion. This is academic gold; engaging seriously with dissent is what raises B-grade to A-grade.\n5. **Axiom blog (Nov 2023): \"The Right To Be Forgotten vs Audit Trail Mandates\"** \u2014 named the conflict explicitly. The conflict is recognised; my contribution is the cross-border EHDS-specific solution.\n\nThis means **Reframe REF-011: my contribution is NOT \"inventing crypto-erasure\" \u2014 it is \"applying crypto-erasure-by-invalidation to the specific cross-border EHDS audit-trail problem under EHDS, GDPR Article 17(3)(b), and TEHDAS2 Article 68(12).\"** This is more defensible than the original framing.\n\nNow drafting Section 2.3.\n\n---\n\n# Chapter 2 \u2014 Literature Review and Theoretical Foundation (continued)\n\n## 2.3 The Audit-Retention vs Right-to-Erasure Tension\n\n(target 1,200 words)\n\nThe thesis is built around a specific structural tension between three retention regimes that operate concurrently on the same cross-border health data event. Each regime is grounded in primary sources \u2014 a technical integration profile, a binding regulation, and an evolving Joint Action specification. None resolves the others. This section examines each regime in turn, then characterises the resulting conflict and the policy commentary that has begun to surface around it.\n\nThe first regime is technical. The IHE Audit Trail and Node Authentication (ATNA) profile, maintained in Volume 1 Chapter 9 of the IHE IT Infrastructure Technical Framework, specifies the foundational audit and security requirements for nodes participating in interoperable health information exchange (IHE International, 2024). ATNA establishes a Basic Secure Node that must authenticate other nodes, encrypt communications using TLS, log triggered events such as queries, views, additions, deletions, and changes within the security domain, and transmit those audit records to an Audit Record Repository. The IHE specification itself does not mandate a single global retention period, leaving that decision to the realm-specific or domain-specific deployment binding. The eHDSI security framework that operationalises ATNA within the MyHealth@EU infrastructure does specify retention in concrete terms. The framework adopted by the eHMSEG governance body for cross-border NCPeH operations requires audit repositories to retain records for a minimum of ten years, characterised by Simjanoska Misheva et al. (2025) as the binding deployment baseline. This retention floor exists because cross-border audit records support not only patient-facing access logs but also accountability for the eIDAS-based authentication of the requesting clinician, the consent validation step, and the audit trail required under the European Health Data Space patient-rights provisions.\n\nThe second regime is legal. Article 17 of the General Data Protection Regulation (Regulation (EU) 2016/679) establishes the data subject's right to obtain from the controller the erasure of personal data concerning them without undue delay, where one of the conditions in Article 17(1) applies (European Parliament and Council of the European Union, 2016). These conditions include the cessation of the original purpose, the withdrawal of consent without an alternative legal basis, an objection under Article 21 with no overriding legitimate interest, and unlawful processing. The data controller bears an independent obligation to erase in these conditions, irrespective of whether the data subject has formally exercised the right (European Data Protection Board, 2026). Article 17(3) provides limited exemptions, of which two are directly relevant to the cross-border audit context. Article 17(3)(b) permits the controller to refuse erasure where processing is necessary for compliance with a legal obligation imposed by Union or Member State law. Article 17(3)(d) permits refusal where processing is necessary for scientific or historical research purposes or statistical purposes, in so far as erasure is likely to render impossible or seriously impair the achievement of those purposes. The European Data Protection Board's Coordinated Enforcement Framework report on Article 17, published on 18 February 2026 following an investigation involving 32 supervisory authorities and 764 controllers, identified the implementation of retention periods as one of five recurring issues, recommending further practical guidance on how to define and implement retention periods consistent with national legal obligations (European Data Protection Board, 2026; Matheson, 2026).\n\nThe third regime is operational and emerging. The TEHDAS2 Joint Action's draft Guideline for Health Data Access Bodies on procedures and formats for data access, published in September 2025, specifies that personal data must be deleted from a Secure Processing Environment within six months of the expiry of the data permit, under Article 68(12) of the European Health Data Space Regulation (TEHDAS2, 2025; European Parliament and Council of the European Union, 2025). This is a ceiling rather than a floor. After the six-month window, only anonymised analytical results may continue to exist outside the Secure Processing Environment, and personal data are not permitted to remain. The same TEHDAS2 deliverable specifies a five-phase user journey for data access that ends in a deletion event marking the closure of the permitted processing.\n\nThe conflict is structural and operational. When a Norwegian patient is treated under MyHealth@EU in another Member State, the cross-border access event is logged in the foreign NCPeH's ATNA-compliant audit repository for at least ten years. During that window, the patient retains the right to request erasure of personal data under Article 17. If the erasure request is granted, the audit record loses its referential integrity: it points to data that no longer exist. If the erasure request is refused on Article 17(3)(b) grounds, the controller must demonstrate that the retention is genuinely necessary for compliance with a specific legal obligation, which is the recommendation the European Data Protection Board made in February 2026 (Matheson, 2026). When the same patient's data move through the secondary-use pathway and reach a Norwegian Secure Processing Environment under a Helsedataservice permit, the TEHDAS2 six-month deletion rule applies. Yet the audit records of the access, processing, and analytical operations performed inside the Secure Processing Environment remain subject to the same eHDSI ten-year baseline through the cross-border audit chain that initiated the secondary-use request.\n\nA direct technical solution to this tension exists in the literature on cryptographic erasure, though its application to the cross-border health audit problem has not been systematically developed. The National Institute of Standards and Technology's Special Publication 800-88 Revision 2 recognises cryptographic erasure as a purge-grade sanitization technique, in which the encryption key used to protect the data is irreversibly destroyed while the encrypted data remain on the storage medium (National Institute of Standards and Technology, 2024). The European Union Agency for Cybersecurity and several national data protection authorities have begun to recognise cryptographic erasure as a means of satisfying Article 17 in distributed cloud environments, on the condition that key management is auditable and that the keys are demonstrably and irreversibly destroyed (Oneuptime, 2026). The technique is not without criticism. SecuPi (2025) argues that cryptographic erasure introduces operational complexity in row-level encryption scenarios and that auditable key management adds rather than removes overhead, making it inappropriate as a general solution for the right to erasure in heavily distributed systems. Engaging with this critique is essential for the thesis to remain defensible; Chapter 6 returns to it explicitly.\n\nWhat does not yet exist in the literature, and what this thesis addresses, is the specific design of a verifiable invalidation mechanism that operates at the cross-border audit-trail boundary defined by ATNA, the eHDSI ten-year retention baseline, GDPR Article 17(3)(b), and the TEHDAS2 six-month rule, in a manner that is both technically demonstrable and compatible with the operational reality of Norwegian NCPeH and Helsedataservice. The next section reviews the cryptographic literature that informs the design of such a mechanism.\n\n---\n\n## References used in Run 2 (added to citation ledger)\n\nNew citations introduced in Run 2 that need adding to Subsheet 18:\n\n- **IHE International** (2024). *Audit Trail and Node Authentication (ATNA) Integration Profile* \u2014 IT Infrastructure Technical Framework Volume 1, Chapter 9. URL: profiles.ihe.net/ITI/TF/Volume1/ch-9.html. **Flag: G** \u2014 primary standards source\n- **European Data Protection Board** (2026). *Coordinated Enforcement Framework Report on Article 17 GDPR (Right to Erasure)*. 18 February 2026.         "text": "The membership proofs and non-membership proofs are logarithmic in size of the set. RSA based Dynamic Accumulators were 1st introduced in [CL02] , with application as efficient revocation of anonymous credentials. [LLX07] presents definition of Universal Accumulator and efficient algorithms for updating membership/non-membership witness, when accumulator is updated by addition/deletion. Assume a RSA group with modulus N, and a random generator of the group g. Let S be set of integers to be accumulated. Hprime be hash function which hashes integers to unique odd primes. Sp be set of primes resulting from hashing elements of S. Let p_s be product of all primes in Sp. This problem, often overlooked in research, restricts the scalability and practicality of blockchain-based audit systems. To address these limitations, this study proposes BEATS (Blockchain-based Efficient Audit Trail System) \u2013 an efficient approach for verifying specific information within a blockchain. \n\nBEATS leverages a Cryptographic Accumulator, a compact data structure that accumulates transaction hashes, enabling auditors to verify transaction authenticity without exhaustive searches or supplementary systems.     "text": "Critical foundational findings. The cryptographic-accumulator literature is real and substantial. **Camenisch & Lysyanskaya (2002), Benaloh & de Mare (1993), Ren (2022) survey** \u2014 these are the canonical references for dynamic accumulators with revocation. There is also **BEATS (Blockchain-based Efficient Audit Trail System)** using cryptographic accumulators for audit trail verification \u2014 direct adjacent literature for my A4 mechanism.\n\nI have everything I need. Now drafting Section 2.4 with proper academic depth.\n\n---\n\n# Chapter 2 \u2014 Literature Review and Theoretical Foundation (continued)\n\n## 2.4 Cryptographic Approaches to Audit Compliance\n\n(target 1,200 words)\n\nA growing body of cryptographic and systems research has developed mechanisms that address parts of the audit-compliance problem in cross-border and multi-institutional health data settings. The four most relevant strands are trusted-execution-environment-based architectures applied to OpenNCP, blockchain-based audit logging adapted to healthcare, zero-knowledge proofs applied to electronic health records and decentralised identity, and the underlying cryptographic primitives of commitment schemes and accumulators that make revocation mathematically tractable. This section reviews each strand in turn, identifying both what each contributes and what each leaves unaddressed for the specific cross-border erasure problem this thesis examines.\n\nThe KONFIDO project, funded under the Horizon 2020 programme between 2016 and 2019, remains the most directly relevant architectural ancestor for the present work. KONFIDO extended the OpenNCP cross-border health data exchange platform with a holistic security toolkit organised around four technical pillars: photonic Physical Unclonable Functions for hardware-rooted identity, homomorphic encryption for computation on encrypted patient summaries, a Security Information and Event Management system for distributed monitoring, and a blockchain-based auditing component for cross-border traceability and liability support (Diamantopoulos et al., 2019; Staffa et al., 2018). KONFIDO was piloted in Denmark, Italy, and Spain, and the lessons-learned synthesis published by Natsiavas et al. (2021) documented both the technical achievements and the substantive gaps in scaling the approach. Two of those gaps are directly relevant. First, the KONFIDO security architecture relied on Intel Software Guard Extensions trusted execution environments, creating a hardware dependency that complicates broad NCPeH deployment across heterogeneous Member State infrastructures. Second, KONFIDO's blockchain auditing component, designated as a \"disruptive logging\" mechanism in the project documentation, provided immutable traceability but did not engage the General Data Protection Regulation's right to erasure as a design constraint. The audit chain was treated as a feature whose value derived from its immutability, with the conflict between immutability and erasure left unresolved.\n\nA second strand of work has explored zero-knowledge proofs applied to electronic health record access control, with the most direct comparator for the present thesis being Albalwy (2026). The ZK-EHR framework uses a Groth16 zk-SNARK circuit to enforce policy compliance for cross-institutional health data sharing, with three actor roles defined as the Patient as data owner, the Doctor as care provider, and the Researcher as authorised analyst. Encrypted records are stored on the InterPlanetary File System, with on-chain smart contracts handling policy enforcement and cryptographic verification. The architecture decouples authorisation from identity disclosure, allowing a requesting actor to prove they hold valid policy-compliant credentials without revealing their identity, role, or affiliations. The framework reports an on-chain verification latency of approximately 390 milliseconds and a gas consumption of 216,631 units per verification. Albalwy's contribution occupies the cross-institutional space within a single jurisdiction; the cross-border space addressed by MyHealth@EU, with its eIDAS-based authentication chains and its operational coupling to ATNA-compliant audit repositories spanning multiple national legal regimes, is genuinely distinct.\n\nThe Health-zkIDM system developed by Bai et al. (2022) provides a complementary perspective. Built on Hyperledger Fabric with zero-knowledge proof components implemented at the chaincode level, Health-zkIDM achieves throughput exceeding 400 transactions per second in Caliper benchmarking, demonstrating that decentralised identity management with cryptographic privacy guarantees is operationally viable in healthcare settings. The architecture targets identity verification rather than audit-trail erasure, but its performance evidence is informative for the design constraints of any cross-border verifiable compliance mechanism.\n\nA more recent contribution is zkFL-Health (Sharma et al., 2025), which combines federated learning with zero-knowledge proofs and trusted execution environments for medical artificial intelligence training. Clients train locally and commit cryptographic representations of their updates; an aggregator operating within a trusted execution environment computes the global update and produces a succinct zero-knowledge proof, generated using Halo2 or Nova, that the aggregation used exactly the committed inputs and applied the prescribed rule, without revealing any individual client update. Verifier nodes record cryptographic commitments on-chain, creating what the authors explicitly characterise as an \"immutable audit trail\" removing the need to trust any single party. zkFL-Health is significant for the present thesis in two respects: it demonstrates that the combination of trusted execution environments and zero-knowledge proofs can produce verifiable audit trails compatible with healthcare data, and it provides a recent illustration of the design pattern where commitments on-chain reference data held off-chain \u2014 a pattern central to the cryptographic-erasure-by-invalidation mechanism this thesis proposes.\n\nWithin the Norwegian clinical context, the NewbornTime project provides a deployed precedent. Brunner et al. (2025) document the deployment of a Hyperledger-based digital consent platform at Stavanger University Hospital, used between November 2021 and June 2025 in the collection of 1,012 thermal videos of newborn resuscitation events. The platform stores consent status immutably on-chain while keeping personal data encrypted and off-chain, providing a protected API for authorised retrieval and a documented General Data Protection Regulation-compliant withdrawal mechanism. The clinical viability of blockchain-anchored consent infrastructure in a Norwegian regulated healthcare setting is therefore established, although the system addresses consent recording rather than the audit-trail erasure problem.\n\nA fourth strand of work, less visible in the healthcare literature but foundational to the design of any verifiable invalidation mechanism, is the cryptographic accumulator and commitment scheme literature. Benaloh and de Mare (1993) introduced one-way accumulators as a means of accumulating elements from a finite set into a single short value supporting membership testing. Camenisch and Lysyanskaya (2002) extended this work to dynamic accumulators with efficient revocation of anonymous credentials, providing the mathematical machinery for verifiable membership and non-membership proofs in sets that change over time. Ren (2022) surveys the field, covering applications ranging from anonymous credentials and certificate revocation through to blockchain-based audit systems. The Blockchain-based Efficient Audit Trail System proposed by ResearchGate-indexed work in 2024 applies cryptographic accumulators directly to the problem of verifying specific transaction information within a blockchain audit trail without exhaustive search, demonstrating the operational viability of accumulator-based audit verification.\n\nThree observations emerge from this survey. First, the technical components required for a verifiable cryptographic-erasure mechanism for cross-border health audit trails exist in mature form: trusted execution environments at the OpenNCP layer (KONFIDO), zero-knowledge proof systems applied to healthcare (Albalwy 2026, Health-zkIDM, zkFL-Health), blockchain-anchored consent and audit infrastructures with Norwegian clinical validation (NewbornTime), and cryptographic accumulators with revocation semantics (Camenisch and Lysyanskaya 2002 and the subsequent literature). Second, none of these works combines these components to address the specific tension between ATNA-mandated audit retention, GDPR Article 17 erasure rights, and TEHDAS2 Secure Processing Environment deletion in the cross-border European context. Third, the broader policy literature on privacy-enhancing technologies under the European Health Data Space, including the research agenda published in Frontiers in Digital Health (2025), explicitly identifies this kind of governance-and-technology integration as an open research category. The contribution of this thesis is positioned at that named gap.\n\n---\n\n## References used in Run 3 (added to citation ledger)\n\nNew citations introduced in Run 3:\n\n- **Diamantopoulos, S. et al.** (2019). *Secure Cross-Border Exchange of Health Related Data: The KONFIDO Approach*.
